Completed volumes and the DR-cycle Thanks: Research is supported by the DFG-project MO 1884/3-1 and the Collaborative Research Centre TRR 326 “Geometry and Arithmetic of Uniformized Structures.”
Martin Möller, Miguel Prado

Abstract
. We show that the completed volumes introduced by Duriev-Goujard-Yakovlev as an approximation to compute Masur-Veech volumes via Witten-Kontsevich’s combinatorial classes agrees with the top intersection of the tautological class on the double ramification cycle, computable as a coefficient of a Chiodo class. For the proof we describe the components of the double ramification cycle and their excess intersection classes to the extent seen by the top tautological intersection. This gives a recursion computing completed volumes in terms of volumes appearing in a certain set of level graphs, not only for quadratic differentials. It also completes the work of Duriev-Goujard-Yakovlev solving the technically most involved case of strata with two singularities.



[name=graph,title=Graphs and levels,columns=1,columnseprule,options=-s Idx.ist]

1.
Introduction

The moduli space of projectivized
k
-differentials
\mathbb{P}{\Omega\mathcal{M}}^{\otimes k}_{g,n}(\mu)
with zeros and poles of orders prescribed by
\mu=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})
comes with a natural finite volume form. In particular for the case of Abelian differentials
k=1
and quadratic differentials
k=2
these Masur-Veech volumes of these moduli spaces have important implicitations for the dynamics on translation and half-translation surfaces, see
[
39
,
26
]
for surveys and
[
34
]
for interpretations for higher
k
.

A challenging problem is to find formulas for the computation of these volumes, that can be efficiently evaluated and such that the large genus limits can be derived. For the case of abelian differentials, the large limit conjecture
[
1
]
has been proven in recent years
[
11
,
2
,
36
]
. For quadratic differentials the principal strata (all
m_{i}=\pm 1
) was shown in
[
3
]
while the general case is open.

There are three strategies to compute Masur-Veech volumes. First, the initial strategy of counting square-tiled surfaces of Zorich and Eskin-Okounkov
[
24
]
and relying on the quasimodularity of the generating function has sucessfully been used for abelian differentials
[
11
]
and for a low genus data base
[
28
]
, but it will play no role in the sequel of this paper. Second, one may decompose the (half-) translation surfaces into ribbon graphs with glued-in cylinders and count ribbon graphs. Key to make this strategy usefuly is to relate the count of integer metrics on ribbon graphs to intersection numbers of Witten-Kontsevich’s combinatorial classes
[
38
,
31
]
on the moduli space of stable curves
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
. This strategy was implemented for abelian differentials
[
20
,
21
]
and principal strata of quadratic differentials
[
19
,
22
]
. Third, Masur-Veech volumes can be computed as intersection numbers of tautological classes on the multi-scale compactification of the moduli spaces
\mathbb{P}{\Omega\mathcal{M}}^{\otimes k}_{g,n}(\mu)
themselves, proven for abelian differentials
[
11
]
and quadratic differentials without relative periods and conjecturally in general
[
14
]
. In the case without relative periods the class that computes the Masur-Veech volume is simply the top power
\xi^{d}
of the tautological line bundle where
d=\dim\mathbb{P}{\Omega\mathcal{M}}^{\otimes k}_{g,n}(\mu)
.

Ribbon graph completed volumes

To adapt the ribbon graph strategy for quadratic differentials with general odd entries
m_{i}
, Duriev–Goujard-Yakovlev introduced in
[
23
]
the notion of
completed volumes
\overline{\operatorname{vol}}(\mu)
of moduli spaces of quadratic differentials: Instead of counting metrics on Ribbon graphs, they evaluate Kontsevich’s polynomials in terms of combinatorial classes, even though they overcount by contributions of degenerate ribbon graphs. Their main theorem
[
23
, Theorem 2.6]
is (for the case of
n\geq 4
singularities) a recursive formula for completed volume in terms of the Masur-Veech volume and lower complexity volumes.

Double ramification (DR)-cycle completed volumes

To evaluate intersection numbers on the multi-scale compactification one may also consider using a completion. The double ramification (DR)-cycle can be considered as an alternative ’compactification’ of
\mathbb{P}{\Omega\mathcal{M}}^{\otimes k}_{g,n}(\mu)
in an appropriate blowup of
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
having additional components that lie entirely over the boundary of
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
. Chiodo–Holmes
[
15
]
recently proved a closed formula for intersection numbers of arbitrary powers
\xi^{r}
of the tautological line bundle on the DR-cycle, which was conjectured in
[
13
]
. It is thus natural to define the
DR-completed volume
\overline{\operatorname{vol}}^{\mathsf{DR}}(\mu)
of any of the moduli spaces
\mathbb{P}{\Omega\mathcal{M}}^{\otimes k}_{g,n}(\mu)
to be the appropriate intersection number, that is,
\xi^{d}
in the case without relative periods, against the DR-cycle, and to aim for a recursive formula in terms of the true Masur-Veech volume and of volumes of lower complexity moduli spaces.

Refering to Sections
5
and
6
for the precise volume definitions we can state our first main result simply as:

Theorem 1.1
.

The DR-completed volume and the (Ribbon graph) completed volume agree, i.e.,

\overline{\operatorname{vol}}^{\mathsf{DR}}(\mu)\;=\;\overline{\operatorname{vol}}(\mu)

for all signatures of quadratic differentials with
m_{i}>-2
odd for
i=1,\ldots,n
.

In fact, the theorem is not just and equality of two numbers, but a termwise equality of two recursions that form the two constituents of the proof.

The theorem now opens a two-fold way to approach the large genus asymptotic conjectures: Use either of the two tautological class expression for the completed volume to compute its large genus limit and deduce the conjecture in
[
1
]
by inverting the recusion (
1
) below. We give a comparison of the two tautological class expression at the end of the paper in Section
7

Components of the DR-cycle and the excess bundle

The space of generalized multi-scale differentials
\mathcal{GMS}_{\mu}
(also known as DR-locus) is the locus inside the rubber moduli space
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}
where the line bundle
\mathcal{L}_{\mu}
defined as the
k
-th power of the canonical twisted by the marked point with weight
-\mu
becomes trivial. Here
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}
is the stack of (projectivized) rubber curves introduced by Marcus and Wise
[
32
]
. One of the components of
\mathcal{GMS}_{\mu}
is the compactification
\mathcal{MS}_{k}(\mu)
of
\mathbb{P}{\Omega\mathcal{M}}^{\otimes k}_{g,n}(\mu)
by multi-scale differentials
[
6
,
13
]
. The DR-cycle is the Gysin pullback of the section of the relative Picard variety over
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}
defined by
\mathcal{L}_{\mu}
along the zero section. It is a class in
\operatorname{CH}^{g}(\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}})
supported on
\mathcal{GMS}_{\mu}
, see Section
2.2
for details.

The components of the DR-cycle whose image in
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
is full-dimensional are called star-shaped graphs and are used in many places, e.g.
[
25
,
29
,
4
]
for a selection of papers. We complement this by a full structure result of
\mathcal{GMS}_{\mu}
:

•

The irreducible components
\mathcal{GMS}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
of
\mathcal{GMS}_{\mu}
are classified in Proposition
3
, using level graphs
\Gamma
that
impose a global residue condition (GRC) at every level
(Definition
3
)

•

The local equations cutting out
\mathcal{GMS}_{\mu}
in
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}
are products of level parameters and function that specialize to GRCs on
\mathcal{MS}_{k}(\mu)
, see Proposition
4.1
for details.

Our main contribution here is the computation DR-cycle in Proposition
5
as a sum over components of
\mathcal{GMS}_{\mu}
with the corresponding excess bundles to the extent seen by intersection with the top power
\xi^{d}
of the tautological bundle. This combines that:

•

The intersection with
\xi^{d}
is non-zero only for two types of level graphs
\Gamma
,
special star-shaped graphs
and
sun-flower graphs
, as defined in Definition
5
and
5
, see Proposition
5
.

•

The Chern polynomials of the normal bundle of
\mathcal{GMS}_{\mu}(\Gamma)
in
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}
is computed in Theorem
4.9
up to terms unseen by
\xi^{d}
.

The reader interested in the full expression of the DR-cycle as a sum over components of the conormal sheaf e.g. for intersection products with other products powers of the taulogical bundle times tautological classes is invited to complete Proposition
4.2
with the local computation to derive the contribution supported on GRC-divisor and to add the contributions from intersections of
\mathcal{GMS}_{\mu}
-components using Proposition
4.1
.

We conclude in Theorem
5.7
: The DR-completed volume can be written as a sum

(1)

\overline{\operatorname{vol}}^{\mathsf{DR}}(\mu)\;=\;{\operatorname{vol}}(\mu)+\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathrm{SF}(\mu)\cup\mathrm{SSStar}(\mu)}\frac{1}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)k^{h_{\mathrm{ab}}}}\prod_{e\in E(\Gamma)}\kappa_{e}\prod_{v\in V(\Gamma)}\operatorname{vol}(\mu_{v})

over all special simple star graphs and sunflower graphs, where
\mu_{v}
is the signature of all legs adjacent to the vertex
v
, and
h_{\mathrm{ab}}
is the number of holomorphic abelian top components of the corresponding graph
\Gamma
. Here
\kappa_{e}
are the enhancements (also known as prongs or as twists) associated with the edges of
\Gamma
.

Ribbon graph counting and the two singularity case

To prove
Theorem
1.1
for
n\geq 4
we identify the terms in the main Theorem 2.6 of
[
23
]
with the recursion in (
1
). This is done in Theorem
6.1
. In this case the special star shaped graphs cannot occur. Note that a ’volume’ appears in (
1
) for all vertices
v\in V(\Gamma)
, not just those on top level, which correspond to moduli spaces of finite volume differentials. For those on lower level, corresponding to infinite volume (meromorphic) differentials, the volume is to be interpreted as intersection number of the top power of the tautological bundle
\xi^{d}
– and this can be computed to give the coefficients
[
23
]
.

For
n=2
we use the hindsight of the intended completed volume formula as graph sum to solve what appeared in
[
23
]
the ’cumbersome’ (and thus excluded) case:

The overcounting of the Kontsevich polynomial occurs, if the ribbon graph admits
integer metrics degenerating to metrics assigning length zero to some edges and this edges are
static
, i.e. the ribbon becomes bipartite after the edge has been removed. To each such ribbon we can associate a level graph (see Section
6.1
for the precise procedure) and the sunflower graphs are precisely the level graphs associated to ribbon graphs with a non-separating static edge.

We thus need two steps: First, to analyze how the pieces of the ribbon graphs can be pieced together to give a valid configuration of static edges. This is the content of Proposition
6.1
. Second, we need to count in how many ways those pieces can be assembled to form a valid ribbon graph. It is through this count that the ’volumes’ of the lower level differentials occur in Proposition
6.1
. The formulas and auxiliary functions are partially remeniscent of computations of isoresidual fibers of moduli spaces of genus zero differentials
[
12
]
. However, the technical task is cumbersome as the degeneration viewpoint singles out a ’maximal vertex’ that governs the coloring in the bipartite complement of static edges, while the DR-volumes lead to expressions without such a distinguished vertex. Several layers of inclusion-exclusion rearrangements allow for the conversion of the two expressions.

This part is concluded with Theorem
6.2
, the recusion for completed volumes on Masur-Veech side in the case
n=2
.

An example: The stratum
\mu=(5,3)
with
k=2

We conclude with an example of a stratum of quadratic differentials that exhibits both types of graphs.

Acknowledgments

This project started at a conference in Toulouse (July 2025) for J. Hubbard’s 80th birthday, when the authors compared completed DR-cycle volume with values and formulas Elise Goujard and Ivan Yakovlev had obtained in their project
[
23
]
. The authors thank them for sharing formulas and insights, and in particular Ivan for explaining their counting and graph coloring strategies in Section
6.1
. The authors also thank David Holmes for insights on the embedding of
\mathcal{GMS}
into the space of rubber differentials. In particular, part of the deformation theory in Section
4.1
will also appear in a joint project of the first-named author with him and Samuel Grushevsky.

2.
Multi-scale differentials and the DR-locus

We fix a
type

\mu=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})
with
\sum m_{i}=k(2g-2)
recording the tuples of orders of vanishing of the differential. Sometimes we switch to log convention
a_{i}=m_{i}+k
and then use
A=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
to denote the
log type.
Fixing the type also fixes the line bundle

(2)

\mathcal{L}\,:=\,\mathcal{L}_{\mu}\;=\;\omega_{X/\mathcal{M}_{g,n}}^{\otimes k}\Big(-\sum_{i=1}^{n}m_{i}z_{i}\Big)\,.

2.1.
The space of multi-scale differentials

We let
{\Omega\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mu)
be the stratum of differentials of type
\mu
with all of the
n
points labeled. We write
\mathbb{P}{\Omega\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mu)
for the projectivized stratum. More generally, for any
k\geq 0
we let
{\Omega\mathcal{M}}^{\otimes k}_{g,n}(\mu)
be the stratum of
k
-differentials of the corresponding type. For
k=2
we simply write
\mathcal{Q}(\mu)
for the stratum and
\mathbb{P}\mathcal{Q}(\mu)
for its projectivization. (We drop the second index and just write
{\Omega\mathcal{M}}_{g}(\mu)
if we consider the unlabeled case.) These strata are sometimes disconnected. We will not need their component classification except for the following.

If
\mu\in k\cdot\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}
we say that
\mu
is
of holomorphic abelian type
. If
\mu
is not of holomorphic abelian type we set

(3)

N\;=\;2g-2+n\qquad d=2g-3+n=N-1\,,

which will be the unprojectivized resp. projectivized
dimension
of all the components of the stratum of differentials of type
\mu
. If
\mu
is of holomorphic abelian type, then there are
abelian components

{\Omega\mathcal{M}}^{\otimes k}_{g,n}(\mu)_{\mathrm{ab}}
of dimension

(4)

N_{\mathrm{ab}}\;=\;2g-1+n\qquad d_{\mathrm{ab}}\;=\;2g-2+n\;=\;N_{\mathrm{ab}}-1\,,

consisting of differentials which are
k
-th powers of abelian differentials. If
k>1
there are for every
d|k
the components consisting of primitive
k/d
-differentials. They have unprojectivized dimension
N=N_{\mathrm{ab}}-1
. Nevertheless we sometimes just write
N
or
d
to treat the dimension uniformly, if the type of the component is clear from the context.

We next recall the essentials of definition of the space
\mathcal{MS}(\mu)
of
projectivized multi-scale differentials
as in
[
6
]
1
1

1

This space is called
\mathbb{P}\Xi{\Omega\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}(\mu)
in loc. cit. The simpler symbol used here is used there for a relative coarse moduli space, locally the quotient by the twist group
. We start with the case
k=1
. We will use the notion of
level graphs (of type
\mu
)
with notation as in
[
17
,
13
]
to describe boundary strata of spaces of differentials and subsequently also for objects in
{\mathbf{Rub}}_{\mathcal{L}}
over a nuclear base. In particular we denote the
enhancements
(also known as
prongs
or
twists
) associated with the edge
e
by
\kappa_{e}
. We index the levels by non-positive integers, starting with top level zero (hence they are
0,-1,\ldots,-L(\Gamma)
, but level passages and all parameters associated with them are indexed by positive integers. We define for each level passage
2
2

2

This notation differs from
[
13
,
6
]
, where the lcm’s were called
a_{i}
, but it is consistent with e.g.
[
17
]
. It avoids clashes with the usual notation for the log type.

(5)

\ell_{j}\;=\;\operatorname{lcm}(\kappa_{e}:\text{$e\in E(\Gamma)$ crossing the $j$-th level passage})\quad\text{and}\quad\ell_{\Gamma}=\prod_{j=1}^{L}\ell_{j}\,.

A
twisted differential compatible with the level graph
\Gamma
is a stable pointed curve
(X,{\bf z})
with level graph structure
\Gamma
on its dual graph together with collection of differentials
{\boldsymbol{\omega}}=(\omega_{j})
for
j=0,1,\ldots,L(\Gamma)
with orders of zeros and poles at the marked points and prescribed by the
m_{i}
’s and
\kappa_{e}
’s at the edges, see
[
6
,
17
]
, satisfying the matching residue condition at horizontal edges of
\Gamma
. A
multi-scale differential
is a twisted differential compatible with some level graph (of type
\mu
) that moreover satisfies the
global residue condition

(\mathrm{GRC}_{j}):\qquad\sum_{e\vdash C}\operatorname{Res}_{q_{e}^{-}}(\omega_{-j})=0\quad\text{for every component\penalty\ $C$ above the $j$-th passage}

and a
global prong-matching
, as defined below. We refer to loc. cit. for the definition of morphisms and details for defining families of such objects, which we will not need in the sequel.

Prong-matchings and framings

Consider a vertical edge
e
of
\Gamma
. A
local prong-matching
\sigma_{e}
is defined geometrically as an order-reversing bijections between the incoming horizontal prongs (aka separatrices) at the upper end
q_{+}
of the edge with respect to the differential
\omega_{+}
at the level of the upper end and the outgoing horizontal prongs at the lower end
q_{-}
of the edge with respect to the differential
\omega_{-}
at the level of the lower end. A
global prong-matching
is a collection of prong-matchings for each vertical edge.

Equivalently (see
[
6
, Section 5]
), viewing the prongs
v_{\pm}
as tangent vectors at
q_{\pm}
, we can define a local prong-matching as a section

(6)

\sigma_{e}\in\mathcal{N}_{e}:=q_{+}^{*}\omega_{X^{+}}\otimes q_{-}^{*}\omega_{X^{-}}\quad\text{such that}\quad\sigma_{e}(v_{+}\otimes v_{-})^{\kappa_{e}}=1\,,

where
X^{\pm}
are the components of
X
at the two ends of the edge. To get rid of the (auxiliary) dependence of a horizontal direction, consider as in
[
13
, Section 3]
the level differential at the upper and lower end as sections

\tau_{+}\in T^{-\otimes\kappa_{e}}_{q+}
and
\tau_{-}\in T^{-\otimes\kappa_{e}}_{q-}
. Then

\tau_{e}:=\tau_{+}^{-1}\otimes\tau_{-}\in\mathcal{N}_{e}^{\kappa_{e}}
. We can now state equivalently that a local prong matching is a section

(7)

\sigma_{e}\in\mathcal{N}_{e}\quad\text{such that}\quad\sigma_{e}^{\kappa_{e}}(\tau_{e})=1\,.

A framing is an auxiliary finite datum that we add to a differential in order to separate the two ingredients that make up a prong-matching. This serves the goal of decomposing components of
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
into levelwise products in Section
3
.
3
3

3

Terminology is consistent with flat surface terminology e.g. in
[
6
,
9
,
7
]
, where the notion of
framed
differential refers to a differential
(X,\omega)
together with a choice of a (horizontal) prong (i.e. outgoing trajectory) at all (or a specified subset of the) zeros of
\omega
. The same word ’framing’ is used e.g. in literature related to stability conditions (e.g.
[
10
,
8
]
) for labelings of the real oriented blowup boundary of meromorphic quadratic differentials.

Consider a marked point
z_{i}
of order
m_{i}
of the abelian differential
(X,\omega)
and view
\omega
as a section of
\tau\in T_{z}^{-|m_{i}|-1}
. A
local framing
at
z_{i}
is a section
\sigma\in z_{i}^{*}T_{X}^{\vee}
such that

\sigma^{|m_{i}|+1}(\tau)=1
. A
global framing
is a local framing for a collection of marked points that is described by the context. For a twisted differentials this set of marked points will usually be the set of nodes at vertical edges.

Suppose
\Gamma
is a level graph and
(X,{\bf z},{\boldsymbol{\omega}})
is a twisted differential compatible with
\Gamma
. Then, given a global framing for each of the levels of
\Gamma
(at the marked points corresponding to the vertical edges) we obtain a natural global prong-matching by combining
\sigma_{e}=\sigma^{+}\otimes\sigma_{-}
the local framings at the two ends of an edge into a local prong-matching.

k
-differentials

We now recall properties of the compactification of the stata of
k
-differentials by multi-scale differentials for
k>1
, see
[
18
]
containing more details than
[
5
]
. Again we denote the compactification by
\mathcal{MS}_{k}(\mu)\supset\mathbb{P}{\Omega\mathcal{M}}^{\otimes k}_{g,n}(\mu)
or just by
\mathcal{MS}_{k}
. It is the union of components of
k/d
-th powers of spaces of
d
-differentials. By induction it suffices to describe the primitive component
\mathcal{MS}_{k}^{\operatorname{pr}}(\mu)
, which we now present.

We let
\widehat{\mu}
in genus
\widehat{g}
with
\widehat{n}
marked points the type of the abelian differentials that arise as canonical
k
-fold covers of differentials of type
\mu
.
[
18
, Section 7]
defines a DM-stack
\widehat{\mathcal{MS}}(\widehat{\mu})
that maps to
\mathcal{MS}({\widehat{\mu}})
such that the image agrees with the closure of the locus of canonical coverings. These are the differentials
\omega
with the property that
\tau^{*}\omega=\eta_{k}\omega
for some automorphism
\tau\in\widehat{X}
where
\eta_{k}
is a primitive
k
-th root of unity.

The marking of the
n
points only partially determines the marking the
\widehat{n}
points, allowing for a permutation group
G
exchanging in each fiber over the
n
marked points cyclically the markings according to the
\mathbb{Z}/k
-covering group. The quotient
\widehat{\mathcal{MS}}(\widehat{\mu})/G
is not yet the desired compactification of
\mathcal{MS}_{k}(\mu)
, since
\mathbb{P}{\Omega\mathcal{M}}^{\otimes k}_{g,n}(\mu)
comes with a generic isotropy group of order
k
that are not present in the quotient. Adding these morphism to
\widehat{\mathcal{MS}}(\widehat{\mu})/G
gives the DM-stack we want, which comes with a map

(8)

s\colon\widehat{\mathcal{MS}}(\widehat{\mu})\rightarrow\mathcal{MS}_{k}(\mu)\qquad\text{of degree
$\deg(s)=\frac{1}{k}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\gcd(m_{i},k)$},

where the degree is the size of
G
over the degree of the non-representable map changing the generic isotropy group. As consequence of this construction we find:

Theorem 2.1
.

The space of multi-scale
k
-differentials
\mathcal{MS}_{k}(\mu)
is a smooth DM-stack. Its boundary
\mathcal{MS}_{k}(\mu)\setminus\mathbb{P}{\Omega\mathcal{M}}^{\otimes k}_{g,n}(\mu)
is stratified by strata indexed by
\mathbb{Z}/k
-coverings of level graphs
s_{\Gamma}:\widehat{\Gamma}\to\Gamma
as in
[
18
, Section 7.3]
. Each such boundary stratum parametrizes abelian multi-scale differentials of type
\widehat{\mu}
compatible with
\widehat{\Gamma}
and with
\mathbb{Z}/k
.

From this theorem we need to retain that a multi-scale differentials of type
\widehat{\mu}
is the same as a twisted
k
-differential
(X,{\bf z},(\omega_{i})_{i=0}^{L})
of type
\mu
compatible with
{\Gamma}
(together with prong-matchings) such that the lift to the canonical stable curve cover with dual graph
\widehat{\Gamma}
satisfies the conditions
(\mathrm{GRC}_{i})
as above. See
[
5
]
for reformulations of the GRC in terms of the curve
X
.

2.2.
Generalized multi-scale differentials, or the DR-locus

We next aim for the definition of the space
\mathcal{GMS}_{\mu}
4
4

4

Notation almost taken from
[
13
]
, where this symbol is used for the unprojectivized relative coarse moduli space. The space we work with is called
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}_{\mu}
in
[
13
]
, but it is hard to associated with that symbol a meaningful name. In
[
15
]
this space is called the divisor relation locus
\mathsf{DRL}_{\mu}
, which we avoid to reserve DR-terminology to classes in Chow rings.
of generalized multi-scale differentials. For this we work in the categrory
{\mathbf{LogSch}}
of logarithmic schemes (see
[
13
,
15
]
) from which we freely borrow basic terminology and notation.

We let
{\mathbf{Rub}}_{0}
be the stack of rubber curves with outgoing slopes at the marked points all equal to zero, whose objects we denote as

(\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\to B,\,\beta\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{G}_{m,B}^{\mathsf{trop}})
. We denote by

\mathfrak{Pic}
the relative Picard variety over
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
. The Abel-Jacobi map associated with
O(\beta)
and the line bundle
\mathcal{L}
induce two sections
\sigma_{0},\sigma_{\mathcal{L}}:{\mathbf{Rub}}_{0}\to\mathfrak{Pic}
, whose images lands in
\mathfrak{Pic}^{0}
, the subfunctor of
\mathfrak{Pic}
consisting of line bundles of (total) degree
0
. We define

(9)

{\mathbf{Rub}}_{\mathcal{L}}={\mathbf{Rub}}_{0}\times_{\mathfrak{Pic};\sigma_{0},\sigma_{\mathcal{L}}}{\mathbf{Rub}}_{0}\qquad\text{and finally}\qquad\mathcal{GMS}_{\mu}\>=\ \mathbb{P}({\mathbf{Rub}}_{\mathcal{L}}),

where the latter is the quotient stack by the natural
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-rescaling action. A
B
-valued point in
\mathcal{GMS}
is a tuple

(10)

(X/B,\beta,\mathcal{F},\varphi)\quad\text{with}\quad\pi^{*}\mathcal{F}\cong\mathcal{L}(-\beta)\,.

We summarize the main result of
[
13
]
and extend it to the case of
k
-differentials.

Theorem 2.2
.

For
k=1
the space of multi-scale differentials is the
full subcategory of
\mathcal{GMS}_{\mu}
given on objects by

\mathcal{MS}(\mu)(B)\;=\;\{(X/B,\beta,\mathcal{F},\varphi)\,:\,X^{\prime}/B^{\prime}\,\,\text{satisfies ($\mathrm{GRC}_{i}$) for $i=1,\ldots,L(\Gamma)$}\}

where
X^{\prime}/B^{\prime}
is the pullback via any base change
B^{\prime}\to B
such that the family is nuclear.

For
k>1
the space of multi-scale differentials
\mathcal{MS}_{k}(\mu)
is the normalization of the union of components of
\mathcal{GMS}_{\mu}
that meet the locus of smooth curves. The image of
\mathcal{MS}_{k}(\mu)
is the substack of
(X/B,\beta,\mathcal{F},\varphi)
such that for any nuclear base changed family there exists some covering of level graphs
s_{\Gamma}:\widehat{\Gamma}\to\Gamma
such that the conditions
\mathrm{GRC}_{i}
hold for the abelian differentials on the
k
-cover corresponding to
\widehat{\Gamma}
.

Since
{\mathbf{Rub}}_{0}
,
{\mathbf{Rub}}_{\mathcal{L}}
and hence
\mathcal{GMS}_{\mu}
are stacks over
{\mathbf{LogSch}}
, it would be more precise to state that
\mathcal{MS}(\mu)
is a substack of the underlying algebraic stack of
\mathcal{GMS}_{\mu}
. See
[
13
, Section 4]
for the necessary recap on minimal log structures. In order to even make sense of applying the condition
(\mathrm{GRC}_{i})
to an object as in (
10
) we need the construction from the following proof.

Sketch of the proof of Theorem
2.2
.

Suppose
k=1
and suppose that
X/B
is nuclear and with a minimal log structure. In particular the map
\beta
specifies a level graph
\Gamma
. Moreover the ghost sheaf

\overline{\mathsf{M}}_{B,b}=\mathbb{N}\langle p_{1},\ldots,p_{L}\rangle\oplus\mathbb{N}\langle E^{h}(\Gamma)\rangle
is the free monoid with one generator
p_{i}
for each level passage and one for each horizontal edge of
\Gamma
. To build the twisted differential one chooses a log splitting, i.e., a map
\widetilde{\psi}:\mathbb{N}\langle p_{1},\ldots,p_{L}\rangle\to\overline{\mathsf{M}}
, such that the composition with the projection
\mathsf{M}_{B}\to\overline{\mathsf{M}}
is the natural inclusion. One checks that
\widetilde{\psi}(\sum_{i=1}^{j}\ell_{i}p_{i})
is a section of
\mathcal{O}(\beta(v_{j}))
and thus can be interpreted as a section of
\mathcal{O}(\beta)
on the complement
U_{i}
of the top
j
levels of the stable curve
X
. With this identification the collection of elements

(11)

\omega_{-j}\;=\;\varphi^{-1}\pi^{*}\widetilde{\psi}\Bigl(\sum_{i=1}^{j}\ell_{i}p_{i}\Bigr)

is the desired collection of twisted differentials. A different choice of log splitting results in a rescaling of these differentials (and the prong-matchings we) by an element of the level rotation torus and thus represents the same multi-scale differential.

For the converse recall from
[
6
,
13
]
more precisely that a multi-scale differential also comes with a rescaling ensenble, a collection of
edge parameters
f_{e}
for each vertical edge and
level parameters

t_{j}
for
j=1,\ldots,L(\Gamma)
. The map
p_{j}\mapsto t_{j}\in\mathcal{O}(B)
together with the compatibilities among
f_{e}
and
t_{i}
can be used to define a log structure with
\overline{\mathsf{M}}_{B,b}
as above on
X/B
and we set
\beta(v_{j})=\sum_{i=1}^{j}\ell_{i}p_{i}
. Defining
\varphi
is now a matter of unwinding definitions (see
[
13
, Section 5.6]
).

The upshot so far is that for
k=1
we obtain an isomorphism of multi-scale differentials without the GRC-conditions with
\mathcal{GMS}
and imposing the GRC’s on both sides yields the claim. The sketch of the maps in both directions also illustrates that the difference between
1
-differentials and
k
-differentials is nowhere essential besides the definition of the
k
-differential version of the GRC, which in turn depends on the covering
s_{\Gamma}:\widehat{\Gamma}\to\Gamma
, not only on
\Gamma
. After having made such a choice, the correspondence still works and yields the claim.
∎

2.3.
The tautological class and its variants

For
k=1
the projectivized multiscale compactification
\mathcal{MS}(\mu)
maps to the projectivzed Hodge bundle

(12)

\rho:\mathcal{MS}(\mu)\to\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{H}_{+}),\qquad\mathcal{H}_{+}=\pi_{*}\omega_{X/{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}}\bigl(-\sum_{i:m_{i}<0}m_{i}z_{i}\bigr)

which is obvious from the viewpoint of
[
6
]
using the projectivization of the top level differential
\omega_{0}
. We define the class
\xi
to be the pullback of the first Chern class
\xi=\rho^{*}c_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{H}_{+})}(-1))
to
\mathcal{MS}(\mu)
.
5
5

5

This is consistent with the use of this symbol in
[
17
]
, hence also in the sage-package
diffstrata
. Note however that the Masur-Veech volume papers
[
11
,
14
]
use
\xi=c_{1}(\mathcal{O}(+1))
.

For
k>1
there are two tautological bundles in the literature. Replacing in (
12
) the bundle
\mathcal{H}_{+}
by
\mathcal{H}_{k,+}=\pi_{*}\omega^{\otimes k}(-\sum_{i:m_{i}<0}m_{i}z_{i})
gives a map
\rho_{k}:\mathcal{MS}_{k}(\mu)\to\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{H}_{k,+})
and we define
\zeta=\rho_{k}^{*}c_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{MS}_{k}(\mu)}(1))
. Alternatively, the covering space
\widehat{\mathcal{MS}}(\mu)
comes with the restriction of the tautological class
\xi
from the ambient multi-scale space
\mathcal{MS}(\widehat{\mu})
. The two bundles are related by

(13)

s^{*}\zeta\;=\;-k\xi\quad\text{and}\quad s_{*}\xi\;=\;-\frac{\deg(s)}{k}\zeta

see
[
18
, Equation (50)]
.

In
[
13
,
15
]
the class
\eta=c_{1}(\mathcal{F}^{\vee})\in\operatorname{CH}^{1}(\mathcal{GMS})
is defined to be the dual first Chern class of the bundle
\mathcal{F}
in the definition (
10
). In
[
13
]
was shown to be a pullback from a ’Hodge bundle’
\eta=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{L}(D))}(1)
, but for a twist
\mathcal{L}(D)
such that
R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathcal{L}(D)=0
. This vanishing is not true for the
\mathcal{H}_{+}
used here, but nevertheless the following compatibility holds and gives a justification for (
12
) in the language of
[
13
]
.

Lemma 2.3
.

The restriction of
\eta
of
\mathcal{MS}_{k}(\mu)
is equal to
-\zeta
. In particular for
k=1
we have
\eta|_{\mathcal{MS}}=-\zeta=\xi
.

Proof.

Suppose
k=1
and let
\mathcal{E}=\mathcal{O}_{X}\bigl(-\sum_{i:m_{i}<0}m_{i}z_{i}\bigr)
. Note that
\pi_{*}\omega_{X/{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}}(\mathcal{E})
is indeed a vector bundle. (This is implicit in (
12
)) above. A quick proof: By Serre duality it is enough to show that
R^{1}\pi_{*}O(-\mathcal{E})
is locally free and commutes with base-change. By Cohomology and Base Change, it is enough to show that
\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}(-\mathcal{E})
is locally free and commutes with base change. But this is obvious, since
\mathcal{E}
is an effective linear combination of sections.)

Now, as in
[
13
]
we get natural maps

(14)

\pi^{*}\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{L}(-\beta)\to\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{H}_{+}

and by adjunction a map
\widetilde{\rho}:\mathcal{F}=\pi_{*}\pi^{*}\mathcal{F}\to\pi_{*}\mathcal{H}_{+}
. To get the desired map we check that
\widetilde{\rho}
is universally injective (even though now the map in (
14
) isn’t). Since
\mathcal{L}(-\beta)
is a pullback from the base, every non-zero global section is non-vanishing on some top level component, one which the map to
\mathcal{H}_{+}
is indeed injective. This now gives the map (
12
). Now
\eta|_{\mathcal{MS}}=-\xi
follows from the standard properties of maps to projective spaces
[
37
,
Example 0FCY
]
.

For
k>1
the same proof works with the bundle
\mathcal{H}_{k,+}
and the map
\rho_{k}
.
∎

2.4.
The Hodge-DR formula

We define the
log DR-cycle
as the Gysin-pullback of along the zero section
\mathsf{logDR}_{\mathcal{L}}=\sigma_{0}^{!}\sigma_{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}_{\mathcal{L}})
. The Hodge-DR-conjecture from
[
13
]
predicts a formula for products of the tautological classes against the log DR-cycle. The conjecture was proven in
[
15
, Theorem 1]
. It states more precisely the following. Let
\epsilon:{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g}^{\mathcal{L},1/r}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g}
be the forgetful map from the space of
r
-the roots of the line bundle
\mathcal{L}
and let
\mathcal{L}^{1/r}
be the universal bundle on the universal family (also denoted by
\pi
) over the domain.

Theorem 2.4
.

The push-forward under the map
p:{\mathbf{Rub}}_{0}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}

\mathsf{DR}_{\mathcal{L},u}:=p_{*}(\mathsf{logDR}_{\mathcal{L}}\cdot\eta^{u})\;=\;[r^{u+1}\epsilon_{*}c_{g+u}(-R\pi_{*}\mathcal{L}^{1/r})]_{r=0}\qquad\in\operatorname{CH}^{g+u}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n})

where
[\cdot]_{r=0}
denotes taking the constant term in
r
.

The right hand side can be explicitly evaluated, as it is equal to a coefficient of a Chiodo-class (see
[
16
]
and in this form
[
30
, Corollary 4]
), to which we refer for the details and terminology)

(15)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ch}_{g,n}(\mu)

\displaystyle:=\,\epsilon_{*}c(-R\pi_{*}\mathcal{L}_{\mu}^{1/r})

\displaystyle=\;\sum_{\Gamma}\frac{r^{2g-1+h(\Gamma)}}{|\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)|}\sum_{w\in W(\Gamma,r)}c_{\Gamma,*}\prod_{e\in E(\Gamma)}\mathcal{C}_{e}\prod_{v\in V(\Gamma)}\mathcal{C}_{v}\,\,\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{C}_{i}

Here the first sum is over all stable graphs
\Gamma
, the second is over all admissible weightings mod
r
, denoted by
W(\Gamma,r)
and
c_{\Gamma}
is the clutching map for
\Gamma
. The edge, vertex and leg factors are respectively given by (if
e=(h,h^{\prime})
is the decomposition into two half-edges)

(16)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{e}

\displaystyle=\;\Bigl(1-\exp\Bigl(\sum_{m\geq 1}(-1)^{m}\frac{B_{m}(w(h)/r)}{m(m+1)}(\psi_{h}^{m}-(-\psi_{h})^{m}\Bigr)\Bigr)/(\psi_{h}+\psi_{h^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{v}

\displaystyle=\;\exp\Bigl(\sum_{m\geq 1}(-1)^{m}\frac{B_{m}(k/r)}{m(m+1)}\kappa_{m}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{i}

\displaystyle=\;\exp\Bigl(\sum_{m\geq 1}(-1)^{m-1}\frac{B_{m}(a_{i}/r)}{m(m+1)}\psi_{i}^{m}\Bigr)

as elements in
\operatorname{CH}^{*}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n})
. Here
B_{n}
denotes the
n
-the Bernoulli polynomial.

We will be most interested in the case
u=2g-3+n
, which agress with the dimension of the stratum (except for the holomorphic abelian case), and in which case
the DR-cycle is a
0
-cycle. We thus abbreviate (including the holomorphic abelian case)

(17)

\mathsf{DR}(\mu)\;=\;[\mathsf{DR}_{\mathcal{L}_{\mu},2g-3+n}]\qquad\in\operatorname{CH}^{0}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n})\,.

3.
The support of
\mathcal{GMS}

In order to decompose the support
\mathcal{GMS}
we define a collection of natural substacks indexed by level graphs
\Gamma
. For certain
\Gamma
, those which
impose a GRC at every level passage
we will show in Proposition
3
that the substacks
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
define a union of irreducible components of
\Gamma
. As technical tool for the proofs and to split
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
(up to commensurability) into a product of level-wise stacks we recast the notion of
framing
, common in language of differentials, in the context of
\mathcal{GMS}
.

Frozen level passages

Fix a level graph
\Gamma
. Its level passages will be the
frozen level passages
of the subfunctor
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
we now define.

Recall that for any level graph
\Delta
there is the operation of
undegenerating
the
i
-th level passage, contracting all the edges crossing only this level passage and moving (and merging, if connected by an edge) all vertices on the level right above and below this passage to one level. We denote by
\delta_{i}(\Delta)
the result of undegenerating the
i
-th passage, a graph with
L(\Delta)-1
levels below zero. The operation of undegeneration at different level passages commute and we denote by
\delta_{J}(\Delta)
for any subset
J\subset\{1,\ldots,L(\Gamma)\}
the result of undegenerating all level passages in
J
.

Suppose that
\Delta
is a degeneration of
\Gamma
, more precisely that
\Gamma=\delta_{J}(\Delta)
. We the call the level passages in
J
the
additional level passages
and the level passages in
\{1,\ldots,L(\Delta)\}\setminus J
the
frozen level passages
of
\Delta
(
with respect to
\Gamma
).

Definition 3.1
.

The stack of
\Gamma
-frozen multi-scale differentials
is defined to be the fibered category

(18)

\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)\subset\mathcal{GMS}

consisting of the objects
(X/B,\beta,\mathcal{F},\varphi)\in\mathcal{GMS}(B)
such that for the pullback any nuclear family
X^{\prime}/B^{\prime}
obtained via any base change has a level graph
\Delta=\Delta(B^{\prime})
which is a degeneration of
\Gamma
and such that
(\mathrm{GRC}_{i})
holds whenever
i
is an additional level passage of
\Delta
with respect to
\Gamma
and such that the level parameters satisfy
t_{i}^{\ell_{i}}=0
for every frozen level passage on any pullback of
X/B
to a nuclear family.

In this language
\mathcal{MS}=\mathcal{GMS}(\bullet)
is the subfunctor where the trivial graph is frozen. We denote by
\iota_{\Gamma}:\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)\to\mathcal{GMS}
the natural inclusion.

Proposition 3.2
.

For each level graph
\Gamma
the category of
\Gamma
-frozen multi-scale differentials
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
is a proper algebraic substack of
\mathcal{GMS}
. Moreover
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
is a union of irreducible substacks of
\mathcal{GMS}
.

The reduced substacks

We
define

\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{red}}
to be locally the substack of
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
cut of by
t_{i}=0
for each level
i
that is a frozen level passage (or equivalently: the subspace where all edges in
\Gamma
persist as nodes). This symbol is justified by:

Proposition 3.3
.

The substack
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{red}}
is smooth, in particular reduced.

Not all level graphs
\Gamma
can indeed serve as frozen levels, as the level passages might not be frozen, i.e. there exist deformations smoothing that level passage. This happens precisely, if the following conditions is met.

Definition 3.4
.

If
k=1
we say that
\Gamma

weakly imposes a GRC at level
j
if the twisted differentials compatible with
\widehat{\Gamma}
that satisfy
(\mathrm{GRC}_{j})
form a subspace of positive codimension inside the space of twisted differentials. If
k>1
we require the analogous condition on the cover: For any choice of
\mathbb{Z}/k
-coverings of level graphs
s_{\Gamma}:\widehat{\Gamma}\to\Gamma
as in Theorem
2.1
the twisted differentials compatible with
\widehat{\Gamma}
that satisfy
(\mathrm{GRC}_{j})
form a subspace of positive codimension inside the space of twisted differentials which are
\tau
-eigendifferentials
\tau^{*}\omega=\eta_{k}\omega
.

We say that
\Gamma

imposes a GRC at level
j
, if it does so weakly and if moreover there is an edge crossing precisely the
j
-th level passage, i.e, connecting level
-j
to level
-j+1
.

A chain of four vertices on levels
0,-1,0,-2
respectively with top levels holomorphic is an example of a graph that weakly imposes a GRC at all levels, but that does not (strictly) impose GRCs at all levels.

In any case we call a level-graph
full of GRCs
if it imposes a GRC at every level passage.

Proposition 3.5
.

The stack
\mathcal{GMS}
is the scheme-theoretic union of
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
with
\Gamma
running over the set of level graphs such that
\Gamma
imposes a GRC at each of its level passages.

We remark that the number of level passages such that
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
occurs in this decomposition is not bounded as
g
grows. To see this, take
\Gamma
starting with a chain of one vertex at each level below zero, with all markings on the very bottom, and each of the vertices connected to a pair of holomorphic abelian vertices on one level higher.

For the proof we use the multi-scale viewpoint on
\mathcal{GMS}
. An alternative proof should be possible using the deformation theory recalled in Section
4.1
and the deformation obstructions imposed by the residue conditions in
[
29
, Lemma 4.11]
.

Framings

We start with the definition of the auxiliary space
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)_{f}
of
framed generalized multi-scale differentials with frozen level graph
\Gamma
. It is the finite cover of
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
where all the edges of
\Gamma
are labeled and moreover at each level we choose a global framing
\sigma^{\pm}_{e}
as defined in Section
2.1
for the set of upper and lower ends vertical edges of
\Gamma
with the following property: Given
(X,\beta,\varphi)\in\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
, then
(X,{\bf z},{\boldsymbol{\omega}},(\sigma^{\pm}_{e})_{e\in E(\Gamma)})
is a preimage in
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)_{f}
if there exists a representative
(X,{\bf z},{\boldsymbol{\omega}},(\sigma_{e})_{e})
of the prong-matched twisted differential (or equivalently: there exists a log-splitting) such that for each edge
e
the framings of the preimage point is related to the local prong matching at
e
by

(19)

\sigma_{e}\;=\;\sigma_{\mathrm{e}}^{+}\otimes\sigma^{-}_{e}\,.

Since there are only finitely many pairs of framings for a given
\sigma_{e}
that satisfy (
19
), we deduce that forgetful map
c_{\Gamma}:\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)_{f}\to\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
(obviously being proper) is a finite map.

We define the substack
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)_{f}^{\mathrm{red}}\subset\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)_{f}
by imposing
t_{i}=0
just as in the unframed situation.

Level-wise splittings

The boundary divisors of the moduli space of multi-scale differentials
\mathcal{MS}
are commensurable to a product, see
[
17
, Section 3]
. Essentially the same construction works for the components of
\mathcal{GMS}
. For a given
\Gamma
we let
\mu^{[i]}
be the generalized type (in the sense of
[
17
, Section 5]
of level
-i
, i.e. parameterizing possibly disconnected curves, one for each vertex at level
i
, together with differentials with zeros and poles of orders as prescribed by the half-edges (markings and edges) adjacent to those vertices.

Note the difference to the description of boundary divisors in
\mathcal{MS}
: For any set
\mathfrak{R}
of residue conditions (as in
[
17
, Section 3]
), i.e. subsets of the set of poles, such that for each subset the sum of residues is required to add up to zero) we let
\mathcal{MS}({\mu^{[i]}})^{\mathfrak{R}}\subset\mathcal{MS}({\mu^{[i]}})
be the subspace of differentials that obeys these residue conditions. These spaces appear in the commensurability diagram for boundary divisors of
\mathcal{MS}
(
[
17
, Proposition 4.4]
), but no superscript
\mathfrak{R}
appears on the bottom left of (
20
).

Proposition 3.6
.

Let
\Gamma
be a level graph that imposes a GRC at every level. Then
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
is commensurable to the product of its level-wise strata, i.e. there is a diagram

(20)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{red}}_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)_{f}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{i=0}^{L}\mathcal{MS}({\mu^{[i]}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{\Gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c_{\Gamma}}

where
p_{\Gamma}
and
c_{\Gamma}
are finite maps.

The top horizontal map is locally the quotient map by the ideal generated by the level parameters
t_{i}
, which are nilpotent of order
\ell_{i}
in
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)_{f}
. Moreover,

(21)

\frac{\deg(p_{\Gamma})}{\deg(c_{\Gamma})}\;=\;\frac{\prod_{e}\kappa_{e}}{|\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)|\ell_{\Gamma}}\,.

We conclude the description of
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
with an easy general dimension observation. A vertex
v\in V(\Gamma)
is called
holomorphic abelian
if all the half-edges adjacent to it have a label in
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and divisible by
k
. Generalizing this, we call a subset of vertices
v\subset V(\Gamma)
which is connected through horizontal edges (only)
holomorphic abelian (horizontally connected) component
if all the half-edges adjacent to besides the horizontal edges it have a label in
\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
and divisible by
k
. We write
V(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{ab}}
for the set and write

(22)

h_{\textrm{ab}}\;=\;|V(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{ab}}|

for number of holomorphic abelian components in
\Gamma
. They are obviously local maxima of
\Gamma
. A component
C
of
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
determines a subset

(23)

V(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{ab}}_{C}\,\subseteq\,V(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{ab}}

of top level vertices where the differential actually is a
k
-th power of an abelian differential.

Lemma 3.7
.

The components of
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
have projectivized dimensions

(24)

d_{\Gamma}:=\dim(\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma))\;=\;d-L(\Gamma)+|V(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{ab}}_{C}|\,.

in the range
0\leq|V(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{ab}}_{C}|\leq h_{\textrm{ab}}
.
The lower bound is always attained for some component if
k>1
. The upper bound is attained if and only if each holomorphic abelian vertex has an abelian component.

Proofs

The proofs of the propositions will be combined as follows.

Proof of Proposition
3
, Proposition
3
, Proposition
3
, and of Proposition
3
.

The properness in Proposition
3
is proven as in the case of
\mathcal{MS}=\mathcal{GMS}(\bullet)
in
[
6
]
: Under degeneration the nodes at the new level passages are forced to satisfy the GRC by Stokes’ theorem. The additional condition
t_{i}^{\ell_{i}}=0
is a closed condition, too.

The remaining statements of the first three propositions are local, to be verified at every point
x\in\mathcal{GMS}
. Let
\Gamma
be the level graph at this point. The key idea is visible in:

Case: the graph
\Gamma
has just one level passage

Suppose the top level has, say,
b
components that impose a
\mathrm{GRC}
. Take the first component
C
and consider the edges adjacent to this component. For each of those edges the normal form
[
13
, Equation (3.11)]
6
6

6

A priori an element in
\mathcal{GMS}
satisfies the normal formal
[
13
, Equation (3.10)]
. If
f_{e}^{\kappa_{e}}\neq 0
it is shown in
[
6
, Theorem 4.3]
how to change coordinates to obtain the first normal form. If
f_{e}^{\kappa_{e}}=0
the iterative Ansatz
[
6
, Remark 4.6]
needs to be run only for
\ell
steps and is thus easily shown to produce a convergent base change.
of the node
u_{e}v_{e}=f_{e}
and the differentials

(25)

\omega_{0}\;=\;\bigl(u_{e}^{\kappa_{e}}+f_{e}^{\kappa_{e}}r_{e}\bigr)\frac{du_{e}}{u_{e}}\quad\text{and}\quad\omega_{1}\;=\;-\bigl(v_{e}^{-\kappa_{e}}+r_{e}\bigr)\frac{dv_{e}}{v_{e}}

implies that the period of the corresponding vanishing cycles equals
f_{e}^{\kappa_{e}}r_{e}=t_{1}^{\ell}r_{e}
by the relation between edge parameters and level parameters. Stokes’ theorem implies that the sum of those periods vanishes and we conclude that

t_{1}^{\ell}\cdot\sum_{e\vdash C}r_{e}\;=\;0\,.

We conclude that either
\Gamma
imposes a GRC, the nodes all persist, and
t_{1}^{\ell}=0
, i.e, the defining conditions of
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
hold, or the sum of residues vanishes for each components, i.e.,
(\mathrm{GRC}_{1})
holds. (If
b=0
, the first possibility does not occur.)

We need to show that the two components are irreducible locally near
x
. The component where
(\mathrm{GRC}_{1})
holds is just
\mathcal{GMS}(\bullet)=\mathcal{MS}
and this follows from the smoothness of this component, the main result of
[
6
]
.

To show the irreducibility of
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
near
x
we will show locally an isomorphism to a neighborhood of a point in
\mathcal{MS}(\mu^{[0]})\times\mathcal{MS}(\mu^{[1]})\times\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[t_{1}]/t_{1}^{\ell}
. Starting with an element of
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
we obtain the elements in
\mathcal{MS}(\mu^{[i]})
by taking the differentials with the normal forms (
25
) on the nodes, replacing
u_{e}v_{e}=f_{e}
by
u_{e}v_{e}=0
everywhere. Thanks to
t_{1}^{\ell}=0
the differential
\omega_{0}
is actually holomorphic (i.e. without simple poles) at the nodes. Taking the normalization of the family gives the two pieces we need. The level parameter
t_{1}
is anyway part of the datum of the point in
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
.

Conversely, given a pair
(X_{i},\omega_{i})
of multi-scale differentials in
\mathcal{MS}(\mu^{[0]})\times\mathcal{MS}(\mu^{[1]})
, we glue the Riemann surfaces
X_{i}
at the appropriate zeros and poles of
\omega_{i}
to form a stable curve
X^{\prime}
with dual graph
\Gamma
. The family of degenerating curves
X
will be obtained from
X
by replacing the nodes
u_{e}v_{e}=0
with the (infinitesimally) degenerating family
u_{e}v_{e}=f_{e}
where
u_{e}
and
v_{e}
are coordinates that put the differentials
\omega_{0}
and
\omega_{1}
in normal form and where
f_{e}=t_{1}^{\ell/\kappa_{e}}
. With this form, the differential
\omega_{0}
glues to
0=t_{1}^{\ell}\omega_{1}
on the other side of the node.

To show that the two constructions are inverses to each other, we use the essential uniqueness of the normal forms and that the constructions are local. More precisely, the (’Strebel’) normal form depends on the upper (holomorphic) end on the choice of
\kappa_{e}
-th root of unity
[
6
, Theorem 4.1]
. On the lower (meromorphic) end, it depends on the choice of an auxiliary section. To pin these choices we fix once and for all in the special fiber over
x
(which is not altered in the construction, just as the whole locus over
t_{1}=0
) neighborhoods of the nodes and choose in annular boundary collars some reference points that we require to match with points in the ’plumbing fixtures’
u_{e}v_{e}=f_{e}
in order kill non-uniqueness in the Strebel normal form.

To summarize: the reader will recognize that this is exactly the plumbing construction of
[
6
, Section 10.4]
without using any modification differentials but using
f_{e}^{\kappa_{e}}=0
instead. This concludes the proof of irreducibility near
x
. The smoothness in Proposition
3
also follows from this construction.

Case: general graph
\Gamma

We start with a discussion of the candidates for irreducible components near
x
. For the first level passage, we conclude as above that
t_{1}^{\ell_{1}}=0
holds or
(\mathrm{GRC}_{1})
holds. Suppose
\Gamma
has two level passages.

If the second level passage imposes no GRC, we can smooth this level passage and parameterize a neighborhood by the union of
\mathcal{MS}
(if
x
is a point where
(\mathrm{GRC}_{1})
holds) and
\mathcal{GMS}(\delta_{2}(\Gamma))
. To show that this space is irreducible, we use the local identification with
\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[t_{1}]/t_{1}^{\ell}\times\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[t_{2}]\times\prod_{i=0}^{2}\mathcal{MS}(\mu^{[i]})
. This identification is proven combining the proof in the case
L(\Gamma)=1
above for smoothing the nodes in the first level passage with the usual parameterization of boundary neighborhoods by plumbing (without a nilpotent parameter, but with
t_{2}\in\Delta
instead) in the multi-scale space.

If the second level passage strictly imposes a GRC, we compare
\omega_{1}
and
\omega_{2}
as in (
25
) and now arrive at the conclusion that for each component
C
above the second level passage

t_{2}^{\ell_{2}}\cdot\sum_{e\vdash C}r_{e}\;=\;0\,

holds. Together we conclude that
t_{1}^{\ell_{1}}=0
or
(\mathrm{GRC}_{1})
holds, and
t_{2}^{\ell_{2}}=0
or
(\mathrm{GRC}_{2})
holds. Equivalently, we see that all components that meet at
x
belong to one of the four spaces
\mathcal{MS}
,
\mathcal{GMS}(\delta_{1}(\Gamma))
,
\mathcal{GMS}(\delta_{2}(\Gamma))
, and
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
(if both
(\mathrm{GRC}_{1})
and
(\mathrm{GRC}_{2})
hold at
x
, and the obvious subsets, if not). For three of them we have already shown this in the previous case. For the last, we prove a local identification with
\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[t_{1}]/t_{1}^{\ell_{1}}\times\operatorname{Spec}\mathbb{C}[t_{2}]/t_{2}^{\ell_{2}}\times\prod_{i=0}^{2}\mathcal{MS}(\mu^{[i]})
iterating the plumbing construction above over two level passages.

If the second level passage imposes a GRC, but only weakly so, the obstruction by Stokes theorem now applies only to edges comparing
\omega_{0}
and
\omega_{2}
and from which we deduce

t_{1}^{\ell_{1}}t_{2}^{\ell_{2}}\cdot\sum_{e\vdash C}r_{e}\;=\;0\,.

Together we conclude that one of

(26)

\text{$(\mathrm{GRC}_{1})$ and $(\mathrm{GRC}_{2})$},\qquad\text{$(\mathrm{GRC}_{1})$ and $t_{2}^{\ell_{2}}=0$},\qquad\text{or $t_{1}^{\ell_{1}}=0$}

holds. We observe that (if both
(\mathrm{GRC}_{1})
and
(\mathrm{GRC}_{2})
hold at
x
), all components that meet at
x
belong to one of the three spaces
\mathcal{MS}
,
\mathcal{GMS}(\delta_{1}(\Gamma))
or
\mathcal{GMS}(\delta_{2}(\Gamma))
. Using the local irreducibility of these spaces (same argument as above again), this proves Proposition
3
for this case and at this point, which claims that we do not need
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
to describe
\mathcal{GMS}
as a union.

We leave it to the reader to check that for
\Gamma
with an arbitrary number of levels the same pattern continues: Strictly imposing a GRC at every level implies the
\mathrm{GRC}_{i}
or
t_{i}^{\ell_{i}}=0
-dichotomy at every level and imposing
t_{i}^{\ell_{i}}=0
gives a component. In the absence of an edge joining level
-j
with
-j+1
one of the conditions involves a product of level parameters. This implies that corresponding
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
is still locally irreducible (Proposition
3
) but contained in
\mathcal{GMS}(\delta_{j}(\Gamma))
.

To prove the remaining claims of Proposition
3
observe that on one the loci
t_{i}=0
, i.e. on
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{red}}_{f}
we do not need to perform any local surgery at the edges crossing the frozen level passages. This implies that the map
p_{\Gamma}
can be defined globally. The fiber of
p_{\Gamma}
is the set of possible framings at the singularities that stem from nodes of
\Gamma
of the image element in the product of
\mathcal{MS}(\mu^{[i]})
. The degree computation follows as in
[
18
, Lemma 3.6]
.
∎

Chow rings and tautological bundles

An immediate consequence of Proposition
3
is:

Corollary 3.8
.

The rational Chow ring of
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
is a product

(27)

\operatorname{CH}^{*}(\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma))\;=\;\prod_{i=0}^{L}\operatorname{CH}^{*}(\mathcal{MS}(\mu^{[i]}))

of the level-wise Chow rings.

With this identification we denote by
\zeta^{[i]}\in\operatorname{CH}^{1}(\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma))
the pullback of the class
\zeta\in\operatorname{CH}^{1}(\mathcal{MS}(\mu^{[i]}))
as defined in Section
2.3
to
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
. We abbreviate
\zeta^{\top}=\zeta_{\top}=\zeta^{[0]}
and
\zeta^{\bot}=\zeta_{\bot}=\zeta^{[L(\Gamma)]}
. The same as the proof of Lemma
2.3
shows:

Lemma 3.9
.

The restriction of
\eta
of
\mathcal{GMS}
is equal to
-\zeta^{\top}
.

We also need the tautological bundles at each of the vertices of
\Gamma
, or more generally for any subset
S
of the vertices at any level
i
of
\Gamma
.
We define
\mathcal{O}_{\mu|_{S}}(1)
to be the bundle on
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
whose fiber over
(X/B,\beta,\varphi,\mathcal{F})
are the multiples of the tautological differential
\omega_{i}
restricted to the vertices in
S
.
Then, using a bullet point to remind us that we have a bundle on the typically non-reduced
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
rather than on the left column of (
20
), we let

(28)

\mathcal{O}_{\mu|_{S},\bullet}(1)\;=\;\mathcal{L}(D)\quad\text{and}\quad\zeta_{S,\bullet}=c_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mu|_{S},\bullet}(1))

Checking on local generators (as in
[
17
, Proposition 4.9]
) shows:

Corollary 3.10
.

In the case
S=V(\Gamma)^{\top}
the set of top level vertices, the tautological classes on the various spaces agree up to a scalar, i.e.

(29)

\zeta_{V(\Gamma)^{\top},\bullet}\;=\;\zeta^{\top}\;=\;\frac{\prod_{e}\kappa_{e}}{|\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)|\prod_{i=1}^{L}\ell_{i}}\,\cdot\,c_{\Gamma,*}\iota_{*}p_{\Gamma}^{*}\zeta_{\mathcal{MS}^{[0]}}\quad\in\operatorname{CH}^{1}(\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma))

4.
The normal sheaf to
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)

The goal of this section is to compute in Theorem
4.9
the normal sheaf to the subspaces
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
to the extent necessary to evaluation in Section
5
the top intersection numbers with the
\zeta
-classes. We will recall in Section
4.1
some deformation theory needed for the proofs. The statements we aim for are in Section
4.2
. The following diagram summarizes the spaces we will be working with:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}(\Gamma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{red}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{GMS}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{Pic}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\mathrm{red}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}_{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{red}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{\Gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}_{\Gamma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{j}_{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{0}}

We let
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}(\Gamma)
be the closed substack of
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}(\Gamma)
where the underlying level graph is a degeneration of
\Gamma
and where the nodes corresponding to edges crossing a level passage of
\Gamma
are persistent, i.e., cut out by
t_{i}=0
for each level parameter
t_{i}
. By definition the image of
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}(\Gamma)
in
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}
is contained in the boundary. We record the well-known resp. obvious facts:

Proposition 4.1
.

The stack
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}
is smooth and
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}(\Gamma)
is a smooth substack.

The intersection of
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
with
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}(\Gamma)
is the smooth substack
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{red}}
.

Proof.

The first claim is essentially due to Marcus-Wise
[
32
]
, see
[
13
, Theorem 2.4]
for the details to match with the definition given here.

Using the identification of
\mathcal{GMS}
with multi-scale differentials (without GRC) from
[
13
]
is suffices to proof the smoothness of the subspace in that space where the level graph is a degeneration of
\Gamma
and the nodes of
\Gamma
persist. This follows as in
[
6
]
, using perturbed period coordinates (or log period coordinates) on all levels of
\Gamma
.
∎

4.1.
Some deformation theory

We need some notation to describe various subspaces of the tangent space of
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}
. The material here extends the local computations of Holmes-Schmitt
[
29
]
to the log language. (Differences in the statements are due to the fact that
[
29
]
work on a space between
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}
and
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g}
, essentially the space
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Div}}
from
[
32
]
.)

First, recall from Section
3
the definition of the set of holomorphic subgraphs
V(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{ab}}
in
\Gamma
. These are a subset of components
\pi_{0}(\Gamma\setminus E^{v})
a connected component of the level graph
\Gamma\setminus E^{v}
where all the vertical edges have been removed. For
{\underline{v}}\in\pi_{0}(\Gamma\setminus E^{v})
we let
X_{{\underline{v}}}
be the corresponding connected component of the partial normalization

(30)

\nu:\ \widetilde{X}^{\nu}\coloneqq\bigsqcup_{{\underline{v}}\in\pi_{0}(\Gamma\setminus E^{v})}X_{{\underline{v}}}\to X

of
X
at all the vertical nodes, and let
\omega_{X_{{\underline{v}}}}
be its canonical sheaf. Then

\omega_{X,\mathrm{hor}}\,\coloneqq\,\nu_{*}\bigoplus_{{\underline{v}}\in\pi_{0}(\Gamma\setminus E^{v})}\omega_{X_{{\underline{v}}}}\subset\omega_{X}

is the subsheaf with (order
k
) poles along the horizontal nodes, and no poles at vertical nodes.

Second, for any abelian group
(A,\cdot)
and any level graph
\Gamma
with level set
L(\Gamma)
and edge set
E(\Gamma)=E^{v}(\Gamma)\sqcup E^{h}(\Gamma)
, split as usual into vertical and horizontal edges, there is a natural map defined on
level generators by

(31)

\rho_{\Gamma}:A^{L(\Gamma)}\to A^{E^{v}(\Gamma)},\qquad\lambda_{0}\mapsto 1,\quad\lambda_{-i}\mapsto\begin{cases}\lambda_{-i}^{\kappa_{e}}&\text{if\penalty\ $e$ crosses the $i$-th passage}\\
1&\text{else}\\
\end{cases}

where
\kappa_{e}
are the enhancements of
\Gamma
. We define

(32)

H^{1}_{L}(\Gamma,A)\coloneqq\operatorname{coker}(\rho_{\Gamma})\,.

From now one we fix again a level graph
\Gamma
specifying frozen levels, we suppose that
\Gamma
imposes a GRC at every level, and consider a degeneration
\Pi
of
\Gamma=\delta_{I}(\Pi)
where
I
are the additional level passages.

Lemma 4.2
.

Suppose that
x=(X,\beta,\mathcal{F},\varphi)\in\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
has as level graph a degeneration
\Pi
of
\Gamma=\delta_{I}(\Pi)
. Then the tangent space to
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}
splits as

(33)

T_{x}\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}\;=\;H^{1}_{L}(\Pi,\mathbb{C})\oplus H^{1}\Bigr(X,\omega_{X,\mathrm{hor}}^{\vee}(-{\bf z})\Bigr)\oplus\mathbb{C}^{L(\Delta)}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{E^{h}(\Delta)}

Inside this space the tangent space to
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}(\Gamma)
is given by

(34)

T_{x}\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}(\Gamma)\;=\;H^{1}_{L}(\Pi,\mathbb{C})\oplus H^{1}\Bigr(X,\omega_{X,\mathrm{hor}}^{\vee}(-{\bf z})\Bigr)\oplus\mathbb{C}^{I}\,.

We denote by
\iota_{G}
,
\iota_{\Omega}
,
\iota_{L}
and
\iota_{h}
(resp.
\iota_{I}
) the inclusion of these subspace into
T_{x}\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}
(resp. into
T_{x}\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}(\Gamma)
). They correspond to changing the log structure in the graph, to equisingular deformations, and to level opening-up respectively.

Proof.

The deformation given by the first summand fixes the underlying stable curve
X
and the map
\beta
, but changes the log structure. More precisely, recall that the log structure at a node
q
of
X
corresponding to the edge
e\in E^{v}(\Pi)
needs an element
D_{e}\in\mathsf{M}_{B}
with two properties. First, its reduction is the element
\delta_{e}\in\overline{\mathsf{M}}
and, second, image is
\mathcal{O}_{B}
is a smoothing parameter for the edges. With the help of these elements the log structure is then defined by

(35)

\mathsf{M}_{X,b}\;=\;\{(u,v)\in\mathsf{M}_{B,b}^{2}\,\,\text{such that there exists $n\in N$ with $D_{e}^{n}=u/v$}\}

For any tuple
\lambda\in(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{E(\Pi)}
we define a deformation of the log structure by sending
D_{e}\mapsto\lambda_{e}D_{e}
. Two such deformations define isomorphic log structures if there is an automorphism of the underlying monoid that takes one into the other. Recall that
\overline{\mathsf{M}}_{B,b}=\mathbb{N}\langle p_{1},\ldots,p_{L}\rangle\oplus\mathbb{N}\langle E^{h}(\Gamma)\rangle
. The automorphism induced from
E^{h}(\Gamma)
cannot be used to relate two deformations of the vertical edge parameters. For each of the
p_{i}
there is a copy of
\mathbb{C}^{*}
in
\operatorname{Aut}(\mathsf{M}_{B,b})
acting by
t\cdot(a\cdot p_{i},u)\mapsto(a\cdot p_{i},ut^{a})
. Unwinding definitions this action gives precisely the image of
\rho_{\Pi}
on the deformations of
D_{e}
. Taking the tangent map to this deformation gives the injection
\iota_{G}
.
(We remark that similar deformations of the horizontal edges are fully compensated by the automorphism induced from
E^{h}(\Gamma)
. For this reason they do not show up in
H^{1}_{L}(\Pi,\mathbb{C})
, only in the deformations of the node.)

The second summand are the equisingular deformations (i.e. touching neither the nodes nor the log structure, but changing each piece of partial normalization
\nu
in (
30
). Standard deformation theory shows that these are described by the inclusion
\iota_{\Omega}
.

Finally, the (non-canonical, depending on choices of local coordinates) inclusion
\iota_{L}
and
\iota_{h}
arise from opening the nodes (or lifting those for
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g})
. Obviously only the restriction to
\mathbb{C}^{I}
is tangent to
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}(\Gamma)
.

For the injectivity of the sum of those four maps observe that precisely the image of
\iota_{G}
is the kernel of the tangent map of the forgetful map to
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g}
. For the other three, the injectivity is obvious on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g}
. That the images of these maps span the tangent space follows from a dimension count.
∎

Next we aim to characterize the tangent space to a component
C
of
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
inside this space. For this purpose let

(36)

b:\operatorname{T}_{x}\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}\to\operatorname{T}_{x}^{\mathrm{rel}}\mathfrak{Pic}\subseteq\operatorname{T}_{x}\mathfrak{Pic}

be the difference of the tangent maps to the sections
\sigma_{0}
and
\sigma_{\mathcal{L}}
at the point
x
. Its image lies in the relative tangent space, the kernel of the tangent map
\operatorname{T}\mathfrak{Pic}\to\operatorname{T}\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}
to the forgetful map.

Moreover we let
b_{G}\coloneqq b\circ\iota_{G}
and
b_{\Omega}\coloneqq b\circ\iota_{\Omega}
and decorate with a superscript
\Gamma
the restrictions to the tangent space of
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}(\Gamma)
. To compute the image and cokernel of
b
we will work with the dual map and identify its kernel. A key observation is that the identification of the kernel with
\Omega^{\max}
in the following lemma holds for every point in
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
, whether the level graph is
\Gamma
or a degeneration of it.

Lemma 4.3
.

The kernel of
b_{G}^{\vee}
and hence also the kernel of
(b_{G}^{\Gamma})^{\vee}
is equal to
H^{0}(X,\omega_{X,\mathrm{hor}})
, embedded into
H^{0}(X,\omega_{X})
via the map

(37)

\bigoplus_{{\underline{v}}\in\pi_{0}(\Gamma\setminus E^{v})}H^{0}(X_{{\underline{v}}},\omega_{X_{{\underline{v}}}})\hookrightarrow H^{0}(X,\omega_{X})\,,

yet equivalently is the space of sections of
\omega_{X}
whose residue vanishes at every vertical node.

Moreover, the kernel of
b_{G}^{\vee}\oplus b_{\Omega}^{\vee}
and hence also the kernel of
(b_{G}^{\Gamma})^{\vee}\oplus(b_{\Omega}^{\Gamma})^{\vee}
is given by the image of

(38)

\Omega_{\mathrm{ab},C}\coloneqq\oplus_{{\underline{v}}\in V(\Gamma)_{C}^{\mathrm{ab}}}\,\mathbb{C}\cdot\omega^{1/k}|_{X_{{\underline{v}}}}\hookrightarrow H^{0}(X,\omega_{X})\,.

In the last statement observe that for
{\underline{v}}\in V(\Gamma)_{C}^{\mathrm{ab}}
the
k
-th root of
\omega
exists, even globally on the stratum.

Proof.

The statements about
b_{G}
and
b_{\Omega}
are the content of
[
29
, Lemma 4.1, 4.6 and 4.9]
. Since the restriction to the tangent space
\operatorname{T}_{x}\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}(\Gamma)
only restricts from
L_{\Delta}
to
L_{I}
but not the other summands, the same holds for
b_{G}^{\Gamma}
and
b_{\Omega}^{\Gamma}
.
∎

To match with earlier notation, we remark that
h^{\mathrm{ab}}=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\Omega^{\mathrm{ab}}
if in the component
C
all holomorphic local maxima of
\Delta
with labels divisible by
k
carry indeed
k
-th powers of abelian differentials.

Corollary 4.4
.

The tangent space
T_{x}\mathcal{GMS}
at
x=(X,\beta,\mathcal{F},\varphi)
belonging to the component
C
of
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
is contained in the kernel of the map

(39)

b_{\mathrm{ab},C}:T_{x}\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}\to\Omega^{\vee}_{\mathrm{ab},C}\,.

Consequently, the tangent space to
T_{x}\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}(\Gamma)
is also contained in this kernel.

Proof.

This is a consequence of the dualization computation

(40)

(\operatorname{coker}(b))^{\vee}\;=\;\ker(b^{\vee})\subseteq\ker(b_{\Omega}^{\vee})\cap\ker(b_{G}^{\vee})\;=\;\ker(b_{\Omega}^{\vee}\oplus b_{G}^{\vee})\,.

and Lemma
4.1
.
∎

The following propositions complete the local picture of the components of
\mathcal{GMS}
inside
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}
. They are not necessary for volume computations, but relevant for determining the full excess intersection class.

Proposition 4.5
.

For a graph
\Gamma
which is full of GRC’s the restriction
\mathfrak{j}_{\Gamma}
of the closed embedding
\mathfrak{j}_{0}:\mathcal{GMS}\to\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}
to
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
is a regular embedding.

Proof.

We adapt the usual strategy to show that an embedding of a smooth scheme into an ambient smooth scheme is regular, compare
[
37
, Lemma 10.106.4]
. We tacitly fix a component
C
of
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
. Let
R
be the (regular) local ring of
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}
in a neighborhood of a given point
x
of
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
. We aim to show that there is a (minimal) set of generators
f_{1},\ldots,f_{3g-3+n}
of the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}
of
R
such that the ideal
I
of
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
in
R
is cut out by
f_{1},\ldots,f_{c}
where
c=g-|V_{C}^{\mathrm{ab}}|+L(\Gamma)
. We let
J
be the ideal defining
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{red}}
in
R
. Let
\overline{\mathfrak{m}}=\mathfrak{m}/J
be the maximal ideal in
R/J
. Since

(41)

\displaystyle\dim_{\mathbb{C}}((J+\mathfrak{m})/\mathfrak{m}^{2})

\displaystyle=\;\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\mathfrak{m}/\mathfrak{m}^{2}-\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\overline{\mathfrak{m}}/\overline{\mathfrak{m}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\;\dim\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}-\dim\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{red}}\;=\;g-|V_{C}^{\mathrm{ab}}|+L(\Gamma)\;=\;c\,,

we may choose
f_{1},\ldots,f_{c}\in J
whose images in
\mathfrak{m}/\mathfrak{m}^{2}
are linearly independent, and more precisely we may choose
f_{i}=t_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,L(\Gamma)
. Since
J=\langle I,t_{1},\ldots,t_{L}\rangle
by definition of
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{red}}
we may choose
f_{i}\in I
for
i=L+1,\ldots,c
. We complement these generators by
f_{c+1},\ldots,f_{3g-3+n}
to get a minimal system of generators of
\mathfrak{m}
. As in loc. cit. it follows that
J=\langle f_{1},\ldots,f_{c}\rangle.
. Now

(42)

I_{0}\,:=\,\langle f_{1}^{\ell_{1}},\ldots,f_{L}^{\ell_{K}},f_{L+1},\ldots,f_{c}\rangle\subseteq I.

To see that they are equal we need to use that the powers
\ell_{i}
of
t_{i}
are minimal to define
\mathcal{GMS}
. More precisely, suppose that
y\in I
were an element that exhibits (
42
) to be strict. Since
y\in J
we may without loss of generality assume

y\;=\;\sum_{i=1}^{L}\sum_{j_{1}=1}^{\ell_{1}-1}\cdots\sum_{j_{L}=1}^{\ell_{L}-1}a_{i,{\bf j}}\prod_{i}t_{i}^{j_{i}},\qquad{\bf j}=(j_{1},\ldots,j_{L})

with
a_{i,{\bf j}}\in\langle f_{L+1},\ldots,f_{c}\rangle
and some
a_{i,{\bf j}}\neq 0
for some
(i,{\bf j})
. We may extend the deformations of the twisted differentials at each level (prescribed by the
a_{i,{\bf j}}
) in a arbitrary way to order
t_{i}^{\ell_{i}}
and glue the resulting twisted differentials as in the proofs in Section
3
to a family over
R/I_{0}
(using once again that the residue constraint disappears if we glue to order at most
t_{i}^{\ell_{i}}
). This shows that such an element
y
does not belong to
I
.
∎

From this proposition we deduce the following statement, stating that components of
\mathcal{GMS}
intersect ’as transversely as possible’. We state the first non-trivial case. The general case (with more levels and more components, see the “Case: general graph
\Gamma
” in Section
3
) can be dealt with similarly.

Proposition 4.6
.

Suppose that
\mu
is a meromorphic signature and that
\Gamma
is a graph with one level passage. Suppose moreover at
x\in\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}
the component
C
of
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
and
\mathcal{MS}
meet, and that
b=|V(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{ab}}_{C}|>0
. Then there exist functions
r_{1},\ldots r_{b}
and
f_{b+1},\ldots,f_{g}
in the local ring of
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}
at
x
such that

(43)

I\;=\;\langle r_{1}t_{1}^{\ell_{1}},\ldots,r_{b}t_{1}^{\ell_{1}},f_{b+1},\ldots,f_{g}\rangle

generates the ideal of
\mathcal{GMS}\subset\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}
near
x
, while

r_{1},\ldots,r_{b},f_{b+1},\ldots,f_{g}
is a regular system for
\mathcal{MS}
and

t_{1}^{\ell_{1}},f_{b+1},\ldots,f_{g}
is a regular system for the component
C
of

\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
.

In particular
\mathfrak{j}_{0}:\mathcal{GMS}\to\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}
is a regular embedding near
x
if and only if
b=1
.

The restrictions to
\mathcal{GMS}
of the
r_{i}
are the sums of residues at the edges to the various components as in the condition
(\mathrm{GRC}_{1})
. It would be interesting to have a geometric interpretation on the whole (Zariski neighborhood of
x
in)
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}
.

Proof.

Let
r_{1},\ldots r_{b}
be the residue sums that appear in
(\mathrm{GRC}_{1})
. These are functions on
\mathcal{GMS}
near
x
. Since
\mathcal{GMS}
is a substack, these are restrictions of functions on
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}
that we denote by the same letter.
Since we suppose
\mu
is a meromorphic signature these functions are indeed independent and part of the (“perturbed period”) coordinate system both on
\mathcal{MS}
and on
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
.

From Proposition
4.1
we take the functions
f_{i}
such that
t_{1}^{\ell_{1}},f_{b+1},\ldots,f_{g}
is a regular sequence for
C
and
t_{1},f_{b+1},\ldots,f_{g}
is a regular sequence for
C^{\mathrm{red}}
. More precisely, since the tangent space to
\mathcal{GMS}
has dimension at most (and a posteriori equal to)
3g-3+n-g+b
by Corollary
4.1
, we may choose (using (
41
)) the
f_{i}
as elements of the ideal of
\mathcal{GMS}
at
x
. By the independence of the
r_{i}
there exist functions
h_{i}
such that

(44)

t_{1},f_{b+1},\ldots,f_{g},r_{1},\ldots r_{b},h_{g+1},\ldots,h_{3g-3+n}

is a regular system for the maximal ideal at
x
.

Now we use that any permutation of the elements in (
44
) is also a regular sequence and we start with
r_{1},\ldots r_{b},f_{b+1},\ldots,f_{g}
. By our choice of the
f_{i}
the space
\mathcal{MS}
is contained in the vanishing locus of these
g
elements and a dimension check completes the claim that they form a regular system for
\mathcal{MS}
.
∎

4.2.
Tangent bundles and the normal bundle

We start with an approximate computation of the Chern classes tangent bundle to the multi-scale space. Note that
[
17
]
gives a complete anwer to this question. However the two resulting formulas are quite different.
7
7

7

E.g., the formula given there involves on the small tautological ring without the horizontal divisor, whereas the formulas that follow from Proposition
4.2
involve
\lambda
-classes that a priori do not belong to this ring.
The one here is adapted to subsequently computing normal bundles.

We write
\mathfrak{j}:\mathcal{MS}\to\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}
for the map
\mathfrak{j}_{\Gamma}
in the special case where
\Gamma=\bullet
is the trivial graph. We define
\operatorname{LG}_{1}^{\mathrm{GRC}}\subset\operatorname{LG}_{1}
to be the set of level graphs that impose a non-trivial GRC on lower level.

Proposition 4.7
.

There is an exact sequence of coherent sheaves on
\mathcal{MS}
:

(45)

0\to\operatorname{T}{\mathcal{MS}}\xrightarrow{d\mathfrak{j}}\mathfrak{j}^{*}\operatorname{T}\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}\xrightarrow{b}\mathfrak{j}^{*}\operatorname{T}_{\mathrm{rel}}\mathfrak{Pic}\to\mathcal{C}\to 0

where the cokernel
\mathcal{C}
is supported on the divisors
D_{\Gamma,\bullet}
for
\Gamma
in
\operatorname{LG}_{1}^{\mathrm{GRC}}
if
\mu
is a meromorphic signature or
k>1
. In the case that
k=1
and
\mu
is a holomorphic signature, the cokernel
\mathcal{C}
is an extension of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{MS}}(-1)
with a sheaf supported on
D_{\Gamma,\bullet}
for
\Gamma
in
\operatorname{LG}_{1}^{\mathrm{GRC}}
.

Proof.

The injection of
\operatorname{T}\mathcal{MS}
into the kernel of
b
follows from the definition of
\mathcal{MS}
as a component of the locus where
\sigma_{\mathcal{L}}
and
\sigma_{0}
agree. If
x
is a not boundary point where the level graph
\Gamma
has a GRC, then the image of
b_{\mathrm{ab}}
in Corollary
4.1
has dimension one in the holomorphic and zero in all other cases. This shows that the dimension of
\operatorname{T}\mathcal{MS}
and of the kernel agree. Since
\mathcal{MS}
is smooth this proves the exactness at
\mathfrak{j}^{*}\operatorname{T}\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}
.

In the meromorphic case we simply need to show that
d\sigma_{\mathcal{L}}
is generically surjective outside the union of
D_{\Gamma,\bullet}
for
\Gamma
in
\operatorname{LG}_{1}^{\mathrm{GRC}}
. In the holomorphic case we consider outside the union of those
D_{\Gamma,\bullet}
the surjective map
\mathfrak{j}^{*}\operatorname{T}_{\mathrm{rel}}\mathfrak{Pic}\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{MS}}(-1)
induced by the dual to the inclusion (
38
). It follows from Corollary
4.1
compared with the dimension of
\mathcal{MS}
that the image of
b
there equals the kernel of this map, proving the remaining exactness.
∎

For the case of a general component
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
we first apply this argument to the top and bottom levels to
{\mathbf{Rub}}(\Gamma)
and then deal with the normal directions outside the boundary.

Proposition 4.8
.

There is an exact sequence of coherent sheaves on
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)

(46)

0\to\operatorname{T}{\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{red}}}\xrightarrow{d\mathfrak{j}_{\Gamma}}(\mathfrak{j}_{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{red}})^{*}\operatorname{T}\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}(\Gamma)\xrightarrow{b}(\mathfrak{j}_{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{red}})^{*}\operatorname{T}_{\mathrm{rel}}\mathfrak{Pic}\to\mathcal{C}_{\Gamma}\to 0\,,

where the cokernel
\mathcal{C}_{\Gamma}
is an extension of
\oplus_{v\in V(\Gamma)_{C}^{\mathrm{ab}}}\mathcal{O}_{\mu,v}(-1)
with a sheaf supported on
D_{\Gamma,\bullet}
for
\Gamma
in
\operatorname{LG}_{1}^{\mathrm{GRC}}
. Here
V(\Gamma)_{C}^{\mathrm{ab}}
is determined by the component
C
of
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
as in (
23
).

Proof.

The idea is the same as for Proposition
4.2
. Again, outside the intersection with GRC-divisors
D_{\Gamma,\bullet}
the map to
\oplus_{v\in V(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{ab}}}\mathcal{O}_{\mu,v}(-1)
is induced by the dual to inclusion of differential forms in Corollary
4.1
. The image of
d\mathfrak{j}_{\Gamma}
is obviously contained in the kernel of
b
. To show equality we use the smoothness of
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{red}}
in Proposition
3
and compare dimensions: Observe that
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}(\Gamma)
has codimension
L(\Gamma)
in
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}
, locally being cut out by
t_{i}=0
. The dimension claim now follows from the corollary combined with Lemma
3
.
∎

We continue to denote by
\mathcal{C}_{\Gamma}
the cokernel as in the previous propositions. We define

(47)

\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}^{\top}\;=\;\mathcal{O}_{D_{\Gamma}}\Bigl(\sum_{\widehat{\Delta}\in\operatorname{LG}_{2}\atop\delta_{2}(\widehat{\Delta})=\Gamma}\ell_{\widehat{\Delta},1}D_{\widehat{\Delta}}\Bigr)

to be the bundle summing all the degenerations of the top level with the factor

\ell_{\widehat{\Delta},1}
, the lcm of the top level passage enhancements.
8
8

8

This agrees with
[
17
, Equation (49)]
, not to be confused with the bundle in (
2
).

Theorem 4.9
.

The Chern polynomial of normal bundle
\mathcal{N}_{\mathfrak{j}_{\Gamma}\circ\iota_{\mathrm{red}}}
of the inclusion of
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{red}}
in
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}
can be computed as

(48)

c(\mathcal{N}_{\mathfrak{j}_{\Gamma}\circ\iota_{\mathrm{red}}})\;=\;c(\ker((\mathfrak{j}_{\Gamma}^{\mathrm{red}})^{*}\operatorname{T}_{\mathrm{rel}}\mathfrak{Pic}\to\mathcal{C}_{\Gamma}))\cdot c(\mathcal{N}_{\Gamma})

where
\mathcal{N}_{\Gamma}
is the normal bundle of
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}(\Gamma)
in
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}
restricted to
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
.

If
\Gamma
has just one level passage, then
\mathcal{N}_{\Gamma}
is a line bundle whose Chern polynomial is given by

(49)

c(\mathcal{N}_{\Gamma})=1+\frac{1}{\ell_{\Gamma}}(-\zeta^{\bot}+\zeta^{\top}+\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}^{\top})\,.

Proof.

The first statement follows from the description of
\operatorname{T}{\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{red}}}
in the previous Propositions
4.2
and
4.2
and the definition of the normal bundles.
