The computation of the normal bundle
\mathcal{N}_{\Gamma}
is essentially the same as the computation of the normal bundle of a boundary divisor
D_{\Gamma}
inside
\mathcal{MS}
in
[
17
, Theorem 7.1]
: The normal bundle is locally generated by the level parameter
t_{i}
. The
\ell
-th power of
t_{i}
is given (away from degenerations) as the ratio of the tautological section on top an bottom level, thus the term
-\zeta^{\bot}+\zeta^{\top}
. If the top level degenerates, this ratio statement is no longer true, requiring the correction by
\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}^{\top}
.
See also the comments in
[
18
, Section 4.3]
for the adaptation to
k
-differentials, using the conventions in (
13
).
∎

5.
The excess intersection classes that contribute to the volume

We compute the contributions to
\mathsf{logDR}_{\mathcal{L}}=\sigma_{0}^{!}\sigma_{\mathcal{L}}(\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}_{\mathcal{L}})
that pair non-trivially with
\eta^{d}
. The first step is to single out the relevant type of graphs.

Definition 5.1
.

A level graph
\Gamma
is called a
(
k
-)simple star graph
, if

(1)

it has one vertex on bottom level (the
central vertex
) and

(2)

for each vertex on top level (the
outlying vertices
) all the adjacent legs (the
m_{i}
at marked points and the enhancement
\kappa_{e}
at the edges) have orders divisible by
k
.

Note that a simple star graph might be not of compact type, and there is no restriction to the genus and the markings at the central vertex.

Definition 5.2
.

A simple star graph is called
special
if

(1)

it is compact type,

(2)

the genus of the central vertex is zero,

(3)

the vertices on top level carry no markings,

(4)

and the central vertex carries precisely two markings (or just one marking, if
k=1
and
\mu=(2g-2)
is a single zero).

We denote these sets of graphs by
\mathrm{SStar}(\mu)
and
\mathrm{SSStar}(\mu)
respectively.

Definition 5.3
.

A level graph
\Gamma
is called a
sunflower graph
, if

(1)

it is compact type,

(2)

it has one vertex (the
sun
) on top level, which is connected to every vertex on bottom level,

(3)

each bottom level vertex has genus zero and precisely one marking, and

(4)

for every vertex on top level besides the sun all the adjacent legs have orders (
m_{i}
and
\kappa_{e}
) divisible by
k
.

We denote this set of graphs by
\mathrm{SF}(\mu)
. The reason for this definition is:

Proposition 5.4
.

Suppose that
\alpha_{\Gamma}=\iota_{\Gamma,*}[\alpha]\in\operatorname{CH}^{*}(\mathcal{GMS})
is a pushforward from
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
with
\Gamma\neq\bullet
and that
\alpha\cdot\eta^{d}\neq 0
. Then
\Gamma
is obtained from a special (
k
)-simple star graph or from a sunflower graph by possibly degenerating the lower level.

If
\mu
is of holomorphic abelian type, then more precisely such a graph
\Gamma
is a special simple star graph with a unique marking, i.e., necessariliy
\mu=(2g-2)
.

Proof.

Let
h_{\mathrm{na}}
be the number of top level vertices of
\Gamma
that are not holomorphic abelian. We label the top level vertices, starting with the
h_{\mathrm{ab}}
abelian ones. Each vertex carries a triple of information
g_{i},n_{i},m_{i}
encodding its genus, the number of marked points and the number of nodes attached to it. We may merge the lower levels as this does not change neither hypothesis nor conclusion and suppose that
\Gamma
has just one level passage.

We start with the case that
\mu
is not a holomorphic abelian signature. By Lemma
3
and the hypothesis
\alpha\cdot\eta^{d}\neq 0
the projectivized top level dimension has to be at least
2g-3+n
, which implies

2g-3+n\,\leq\,\sum_{i=1}^{h_{\mathrm{ab}}}(2g_{i}-1+n_{i}+m_{i})+\sum_{i=h_{\mathrm{ab}}+1}^{h_{\mathrm{ab}}+h_{\mathrm{na}}}(2g_{i}-2+n_{i}+m_{i})-1

or equivalently that

2\left(g-\sum_{i=1}^{h_{\mathrm{ab}}+h_{\mathrm{na}}}g_{i}\right)+\left(n-\sum_{i=1}^{h_{\mathrm{ab}}+h_{\mathrm{na}}}n_{i}\right)\,\leq\,2-h_{\mathrm{na}}+\sum_{i=1}^{h_{\mathrm{ab}}+h_{\mathrm{na}}}(m_{i}-1)\,.

Note that the top
\zeta
-power of a holomorphic abelian top level vertex will be zero if it has more than one node. Therefore
m_{i}=1
for
i=1,\ldots,h_{\mathrm{ab}}
, and we abbreviate by

\displaystyle r_{g}\ =g-\sum_{i=1}^{h_{\mathrm{ab}}+h_{\mathrm{na}}}g_{i},\qquad r_{n}\;=\;n-\sum_{i=1}^{h_{\mathrm{ab}}+h_{\mathrm{na}}}n_{i},\qquad r_{m}\;=\;\sum_{i=h_{\mathrm{ab}}+1}^{h_{\mathrm{ab}}+h_{\mathrm{na}}}(m_{i}-1)

the remaining genus, marking and nodes (for bottom level) respectively.

We claim that
1+r_{m}-r_{g}
is a lower bound for the number of vertices on bottom level. This bound holds if
\Gamma
is a tree and continues to hold for every insertion of any further edges, thus justifying the claim.

Moreover, there should be at least one marking for every bottom vertex. Taken together

1+r_{m}-r_{g}\,\leq\,\#\{\text{bottom level vertices}\}\,\leq\,r_{n}\,\leq\,2-h_{\mathrm{na}}+r_{m}-2r_{g}\,.

This leads to the inequality

0\,\leq\,1-h_{\mathrm{na}}-r_{g}

which implies one of the following cases:

•

r_{g}=1
and
h_{\mathrm{na}}=0
: then the number of bottom level vertices is
r_{m}=0
, which is impossible.

•

r_{g}=0
and
h_{\mathrm{na}}=1
: then the number of bottom level vertices is
r_{n}=1+r_{m}
, so each contains exactly one marking and is connected to the unique non-holomorphic abelian top vertex, i.e., we have a sunflower graph.

•

r_{g}=0
and
h_{\mathrm{na}}=0
: then there are
1+r_{m}
bottom level vertices one with two markings and the rest with one marking each. However, as
r_{m}=0
, there is a unique bottom component containing two markings. This case only happens when
|\mu|=2
, i.e., we have a special simple star graph.

If
k=1
and
\mu
is holomorphic, then the projectivized top level dimension has to be at least
2g-2+n
, which leads to the inequality

2g-2+n\,\leq\,\sum_{i=1}^{h_{\mathrm{ab}}}(2g_{i}-1+n_{i}+m_{i})+\sum_{i=h_{\mathrm{ab}}+1}^{h_{\mathrm{ab}}+h_{\mathrm{na}}}(2g_{i}-2+n_{i}+m_{i})-1\,.

The inequality on the number of bottom level vertices now becomes

1+r_{m}-r_{g}\,\leq\,\#\{\text{bottom level vertices}\}\,\leq\,r_{n}\,\leq\,1-h_{\mathrm{na}}+r_{m}-2r_{g}\,.

Then
r_{g}=0
,
h_{\mathrm{na}}=0
,
r_{m}=0
, and
r_{n}=1
which implies that there is a unique bottom component with genus zero and a single marking. This can only happen when
|\mu|=1
and
h_{\mathrm{ab}}\geq 2
due to stability on the bottom vertex, again a special simple star graph.
∎

Inspection of the cases also shows in the situation of the preceding proposition:

Corollary 5.5
.

If
\alpha\cdot\eta^{d}\neq 0
on
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
, then the dimension of the projectivized top level statum of
\Gamma
is equal to
d
(both in the meromorphic case and in the holomorphic case, where we abuse
d
instead of writing
d_{\mathrm{ab}}
.)

Next we observe that this graphs indeed are the only ones that pair non-trivially with the
\eta^{d}
. Recall from (
24
) that
d_{\Gamma}=\dim(\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma))
.

Proposition 5.6
.

The intersection of
\mathsf{logDR}
with the top power of
\eta
is a sum

(50)

[\mathsf{logDR}_{\mathcal{L}}\cdot\eta^{d}]\;=\;\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathrm{SF}(\mu)\cup\mathrm{SSStar}(\mu)\atop\text{or $\Gamma=\bullet$}}\iota_{\Gamma,*}[\mathsf{DR}_{d,\Gamma}]

over contributions from the trivial graph, sunflower graphs and special simple star graphs. Here

(51)

[\mathsf{DR}_{d,\Gamma}]\;=\;\ell_{\Gamma}\cdot c_{d_{\Gamma}-d}(\mathfrak{j}_{\mathcal{L}}^{*}\mathcal{N}_{\sigma_{0}}/\mathcal{N}_{j_{0}|\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{red}}})\cdot\eta^{d}\,.

Proof.

By definition of intersection products (e.g.
[
27
, Section 6.1]
)
\mathsf{logDR}_{\mathcal{L}}=\sum_{i}\ell_{i}\alpha_{i}
where the sum is over the distinguished subvarieties of the intersection product, i.e. over the images of irreducible components
C_{i}
of the normal cone of
\mathcal{GMS}
in
\mathbb{P}{\mathbf{Rub}}
. Here
\ell_{i}
is the geometric multiplicity of
C_{i}
in
C=C_{{\mathbf{Rub}}}\mathcal{GMS}
. By Proposition
3
the distinguished subvarieties are the
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{red}}
for
\Gamma
imposing a GRC at each of its levels, and intersections of such.

Now we apply Proposition
5
. We deduce that among the substacks
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{red}}
only the graphs
\Gamma
listed in the proposition give components of the normal cone where the intersection with
\eta^{d}
may have a non-zero contribution irrespectively of the Segre class of the cone. (Observe that degenerating the lower level of
\Gamma\in\mathrm{SF}(\mu)\cup\mathrm{SSStar}(\mu)
will not give a graph that imposes a GRC at all levels, so these degenerations do not show up in the list.)

We next argue, that no distinguished subvariety contained in an intersection of two such
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma_{i})^{\mathrm{red}}
for
i=1,2
pairs non-trivially with
\eta^{d}
. The level graph at such an intersection has to be a bottom level degeneration of (say
\Gamma_{1}
) a sunflower or a special simple star graph by Proposition
5
. But the graph
\Gamma_{2}
that arises from top level undegeneration the does not impose a GRC at all level, and thus does not determine a component of
\mathcal{GMS}
, contradiction.

Since the
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{red}}
are smooth hence regularly embedded, their contribution can be computed using the excess intersection formula as given. The final claim implicit in the notation is that the geometric multiplicity equals
\ell_{i}
, which can be read off from the local equations for
\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)
in Proposition
4.1
.
∎

For any signature
\mu
(without imposing restrictions neither on the
m_{i}
nor on
k
) we define
intersection-theoretic volume
of a stratum to be

(52)

\operatorname{vol}(\mu)\,:=\,\operatorname{vol}({\Omega\mathcal{M}}^{\otimes k}_{g,n}(\mu))\;=\;\int_{\mathbb{P}{\Omega\mathcal{M}}^{\otimes k}_{g,n}(\mu)}\zeta^{d}

the top intersection of the dual tautological class
\zeta
. We use
\operatorname{vol}(\mu)
in small letters for the intersection-theory side and compare in Section
6
with Mazur-Veech volumes
\operatorname{Vol}(\mu)
. Our goal here is to compute the
DR-completed volume

(53)

\overline{\operatorname{vol}}(\mu)^{\mathsf{DR}}\;=\;\int_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}}\mathsf{DR}(\mu)\zeta^{d}

in terms of intersection-theoretic volumes of strata.

Theorem 5.7
.

The DR-completed volume can be written as a sum

(54)

\overline{\operatorname{vol}}^{\mathsf{DR}}(\mu)\;=\;{\operatorname{vol}}(\mu)+\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathrm{SF}(\mu)\cup\mathrm{SSStar}(\mu)}\frac{1}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)k^{h_{\mathrm{ab}}}}\prod_{e\in E(\Gamma)}\kappa_{e}\prod_{v\in V(\Gamma)}\operatorname{vol}(\mu_{v})

over all special simple star graphs and sunflower graphs, where
\mu_{v}
is the signature of all legs adjacent to the vertex
v
, and
h_{\mathrm{ab}}
is the number of holomorphic abelian top components of the corresponding graph
\Gamma
.

Here the volume of abelian top components has to be taken with respect to its tautological class
\zeta=-\xi
as space of abelian differentials. If considered as a space of
k
-powers of abelian differentials, this introduces a factor
k
for each such vertex (compare with
13
), cancelling the
k^{h_{\mathrm{ab}}}
-denominator.

Proof.

Evaluating the DR-volume on the left hand side we apply
Proposition
5
to first reduce to the summands on the right hand side. For each of those summand we use Theorem
4.9
to evaluate that the Chern polynomial of the excess bundle is given by

(55)

c(\mathfrak{j}_{\mathcal{L}}^{*}\mathcal{N}_{\sigma_{0}}/\mathcal{N}_{j_{0}|\mathcal{GMS}(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{red}}})\;=\;c(C_{\Gamma})\cdot c(\mathcal{N}_{\Gamma})^{-1}

since
\operatorname{T}_{\mathrm{rel}}\mathfrak{Pic}=\mathcal{N}_{\sigma_{0}}
with
\mathcal{C}_{\Gamma}
as in Proposition
4.2
.

We next argue that we can replace
c(C_{\Gamma})
by
\oplus_{v\in V(\Gamma)^{\mathrm{ab}}}\mathcal{O}_{\mu,v}(-1)
when taking the preceding equation
\cdot\eta^{d}
. In fact, there are two cases. First, consider
\Gamma=\bullet
, i.e., the component
\mathcal{MS}
. In the meromorphic (or
k>1
case) the excess class is a zeroth Chern class. In the holomorphic case the only graph that appears in Proposition
5
has an inconvenient (in the sense of
[
33
]
) bottom vertex, i.e., does not appear in the boundary of
\mathcal{MS}
. Second, if
\Gamma
is a special simple star or a sunflower. Then the botton level has no degenerations that impose GRC’s as neither the level-moving in the sunflower case does, nor such exist for the star graphs where there is at most one zero and the rest are (residue-unconstrained) poles. Any top level degeneration of such a
\Gamma
is not a special simple star nor a sunflower, so top-
\zeta^{d}
evaluates to zero there whatever the excess class may contribute.

From Corollary
5
we deduce that the full
d_{\Gamma}-d
-th Chern class taken from the excess class must be supported on lower level. Since the remaining contributions from
\mathcal{C}
are to the upper level, this term must come from
c(N_{\Gamma})^{-1}
, and in fact the only part of the excess class that contributes equals
\zeta^{d-d_{\Gamma}}
. This evaluates to the product of the volumes on the lower level vertices. A standard Segre class computation evaluates the term
\zeta^{d}
as the product of the volumes of the top level vertices.

In the preceding conversions to volumes we have been using the level-wise
\zeta^{\top}
. The conversion to the (global)
\zeta
appearing in (
53
) is responsible for the factor
\prod\kappa_{e}/|\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)|\ell_{\Gamma}
by Corollary
3
. The
k
-power arise from the conversion of
\xi
to
\zeta
.
∎

6.
Volumes of strata and DR-volumes

For any stratum in
k=1
or
k=2
and of finite volume (i.e.,
m_{i}>-k
or equivalently
a_{i}>0
for all
i
) we denote by
\operatorname{Vol}(\mu)=\operatorname{Vol}({\Omega\mathcal{M}}^{\otimes k}_{g,n}(\mu))
the
Masur-Veech volume
in the normalization as explained in
[
14
]
, taking the hypersurface volume on differentials with unitary area.
If the stratum is
REL zero
, i.e., if
k=1
and
n=1
or
k=2
and all
m_{i}
are odd, then
[
35
]
and
[
14
]
relate the Masur-Veech volume and the intersection theoretic volume by

\displaystyle\operatorname{Vol}(\mu)

\displaystyle\;=\;

\displaystyle\frac{2(2\pi i)^{2g}}{(2g-1)!}\operatorname{vol}(\mu)\ \ \text{for}\ \ k=1,\ n=1\,,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Vol}(\mu)

\displaystyle\;=\;

\displaystyle\frac{2^{-n+3}(2\pi i)^{2g-2+n}}{(2g-3+n)!}\operatorname{vol}(\mu)\ \text{for}\ k=2,\ m_{i}^{\prime}s\ \text{odd}\,.

In the case
k=2
and all entries
m_{i}>-2
odd the
completed volume
by
\overline{\operatorname{Vol}}(\mathcal{Q}(\mu))
of strata was defined in
[
23
]
as an approximation to the Masur-Veech volume using counts of integer metrics on Ribbon graphs via Kontsevich polynomials. This completed volume is expressed as the volume of the strata plus the contribution of adjacent strata coming from the counting of integer metrics on degenerate ribbon graphs. The goal of this section is to show that this completed volume can also be interpreted as a sum over contributions from level graphs. In order to state the formula in parallel to the DR-case we define the (Ribbon graph) completed volume in the intersection theory convention by

(56)

\overline{\operatorname{Vol}}(\mu)\;=\;\frac{2^{2g+1}(-1)^{g-1+\frac{n}{2}}\pi^{2g-2+n}}{(2g-3+n)!}\overline{\operatorname{vol}}(\mu)\,

where
n
is even as all the entries
m_{i}
are odd and their sum is
2g-2
.

We will prove two theorems. The first theorem for
|\mu|\geq 3
requires simply a matching of terms and of volume conventions. The second theorem for
|\mu|=2
requires a complete discussion of ribbon graphs and their completed volumes, since this case was omitted in
[
23
]
. Observe that in the case
|\mu|\geq 3
only sunflowers appear in Proposition
5
. This fact is matched by the following formula.

Theorem 6.1
.

For
k=2
and
|\mu|\geq 3
the Ribbon-graph completed volume can be written in intersection theory convention as a sum

(57)

\overline{\operatorname{vol}}(\mu)\;=\;{\operatorname{vol}}(\mu)+\sum_{\Gamma\in\mathrm{SF}(\mu)}\frac{1}{\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)2^{h_{\mathrm{ab}}}}\prod_{e\in E(\Gamma)}\kappa_{e}\prod_{v\in V(\Gamma)}\operatorname{vol}(\mu_{v})

over all sunflower graphs, where
\mu_{v}
is the signature of all legs adjacent to the vertex
v
.

Observe that the last product is over all vertices of
\Gamma
, including those on bottom level, which have higher order poles and where the volume has known no Masur-Veech-type interpretation.

Proof.

The formula presented in of
[
23
, Theorem 2.6]
decomposes the completed volume for a stratum
\mu=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})
with odd entrees with
n\geq 3
and
m_{i}\geq-1
, is

\displaystyle\overline{\operatorname{Vol}}(\mathcal{Q}(\mu))-{\operatorname{Vol}}(\mathcal{Q}(\mu))

\displaystyle\;=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\bf g}=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n})\\
0\leq g_{i}\leq\frac{m_{i}+1}{4}\end{subarray}}\,\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$i$ such that}\\
g_{i}>0\end{subarray}}\,\,\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}{\bf g}_{i}=(g_{i}^{(1)},g_{i}^{(2)},\ldots)\\
|{\bf g}_{i}|=g_{i},\ g_{i}^{(j)}>0\end{subarray}}C_{{\bf g},{\bf g}_{i}}\operatorname{Vol}\Bigl(\mathcal{Q}(\mu-4{\bf g})\times\prod_{i,j}\mathcal{H}(2g_{i}^{(j)}-2)\Bigr)\,.

where the coefficient
C_{{\bf g},{\bf g}_{i}}
is defined in (
59
) below and where
\mu-4{\bf g}
denotes vector subtraction.

First, claim that the decomposition in this sum is in bijection with a sum over sunflower graphs. In fact, the disconnected stratum
\mathcal{Q}(\mu-4{\bf g})\times\prod_{i,j}\mathcal{H}(2g_{i}^{(j)}-2)
describes the top components of two level graph
\Gamma
, where
\mathcal{Q}(\mu-4{\bf g})
corresponds to the non-abelian component (the
sun
), and each
\mathcal{H}(2g_{i}^{(j)}-2)
is an abelian holomorphic principal stratum of genus
g_{i}^{(j)}
. We get a bottom component of genus zero for every
i
such that
g_{i}>0
, where
g_{i}
equals the sum of the genera of abelian vertices attached to that bottom compoenent. This bottom component has a unique marking
m_{i}
and one nodes connecting it to the sun and one node connecting to each abelian component
\mathcal{H}(2g_{i}^{(j)}-2)
for
j=1,\ldots,|{\bf g}_{i}|
. Moreover, the intersection theoretic volume of this bottom vertex
v_{i}
is

(58)

\operatorname{vol}(\mu_{v_{i}})\;=\;\frac{m_{i}!!}{(m_{i}-2(|{\bf g}_{i}|-1))!!}

as computed in Proposition 5.1 of
[
12
]
.

Second, we need to justify that the coefficient given in
[
23
]
as

(59)

C_{{\bf g},{\bf g}_{i}}\;=\;\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$i$ such that}\\
g_{i}>0\end{subarray}}\frac{m_{i}!!}{(m_{i}-2(|{\bf g}_{i}|-1))!!}\frac{(m_{i}-4g_{i}+2)}{|{\bf g}_{i}|!2^{|{\bf g}_{i}|}}\frac{|{\bf g}_{i}|!}{|\operatorname{Aut}({\bf g}_{i})|}\prod_{j=1}^{|{\bf g}_{i}|}(2g_{i}^{(j)}-1)

in fact equals

(60)

C_{{\bf g},{\bf g}_{i}}\;=\;\frac{1}{2^{2h_{\mathrm{ab}}}|\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)|}\prod_{e\in E(\Gamma)}\kappa_{e}\prod_{v\in V(\Gamma^{\perp})}\operatorname{vol}(\mu_{v})\,,

where
\operatorname{Aut}({\bf g}_{i})
is the subgroup of the symmetric group that permutes only places with the same
g_{i}
. In fact, the automorphisms of a sunflower graph have the property that

\frac{1}{2^{h_{\mathrm{ab}}}|\operatorname{Aut}(\Gamma)|}\;=\;\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{$i$ such that}\\
g_{i}>0\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{2^{|{\bf g}_{i}|}|\operatorname{Aut}({\bf g}_{i})|}\,.

Grouping the prongs as those attached to the sun and those attached to the remaining vertices we observe

\frac{\prod_{e\in E(\Gamma)}\kappa_{e}}{2^{h_{\mathrm{ab}}}}\;=\;\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\ s.t.\\
g_{i}>0\end{subarray}}(m_{i}-4g_{i}+2)\prod_{j=1}^{|{\bf g}_{i}|}(2g_{i}^{(j)}-1)\,.

Third, the volume of the top level product stratum is given in
[
23
, Equation (32)]
. Note however that they they normalize the Masur-Veech volume on strata of quadratic differentials with (the cone under) area
1/2
. Converting all these volumes into our (unit volume) convention we obtain that

(61)

\operatorname{Vol}\Bigl(\mathcal{Q}(\mu^{\prime})\times\prod_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{H}(2g_{i}-2)\Bigr)\;=\;\frac{(d^{\prime}-1)!\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{Q}(\mu^{\prime}))\prod_{i=1}^{r}(2g_{i}-1)!\operatorname{Vol}\mathcal{H}(2g_{i}-2)}{(d-1)!}

where
d^{\prime}=\dim\mathcal{Q}(\mu^{\prime})
,
2g_{i}=\dim\mathcal{H}(2g_{i}-2)
,
d=\dim\left(\mathcal{Q}(\mu^{\prime})\times\prod_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{H}(2g_{i}-2)\right)
. Let
g^{\prime}=\sum_{g_{i}\in\underline{g}}g_{i}
. Using the volume conversions

\displaystyle\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{Q}(\mu-4{\bf g}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2^{2(g-g^{\prime})+1}(-1)^{(g-g^{\prime})-1+\frac{n}{2}}\pi^{2(g-g^{\prime})-2+n}}{(2(g-g^{\prime})-3+n)!}\operatorname{vol}(\mathcal{Q}(\mu-4{\bf g}))\,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Vol}(\mathcal{H}(2g_{i}^{(j)}-2))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2(2\pi)^{2g_{i}^{(j)}}(-1)^{g_{i}^{(j)}}}{(2g_{i}^{(j)}-1)!}\operatorname{vol}(\mathcal{H}(2g_{i}^{(j)}-2))\,.

and combining the formula, we conclude the formula (
57
) in the theorem.
∎

The goal of the rest of the section is:

Theorem 6.2
.

For
k=2
and
|\mu|\geq 2
the statement of Theorem
6.1
also holds with the sum on the right hand side replaced by
\Gamma\in\mathrm{SF}(\mu)\cup\mathrm{SSStar}(\mu)
, now also including special simple star graphs.

6.1.
Ribbon graph counting

We summarize the counting procedure of
[
23
]
, define the various types of graphs appearing in their strategy, and show how they relate to the level graphs that appeared in the previous sections.

Associated with any square-tiled (abelian, or here rather) quadratic differential
(X,q)
there is

•

the
(cylinder) stable graph
\Delta
,
9
9

9

these are called
\Gamma
in
[
23
]
, we try to reserve that letter for stable graphs that appear as level graphs
, and

•

a ribbon graph
G
, whose vertices are the singularities of
q
, and whose boundary components in bijection with the cylinders, i.e. with the edges of
\Delta
, and

•

an integer metric on (the edges of)
G
, and heights of the cylinders.

Here the graph
\Delta
is obtained by taking the limit point in the Deligne-Mumford compactification of the geodesic flow applied to
(X,q)
, equivalently the stable curve obtained by shrinking each horizontal cylinder of
(X,q)
to a point. The ribbon graph
G
is a neighborhood of the complement of the horizontal cylinders.

As in
[
23
]
we often pass to the
dual ribbon graph
of
G
, denoted by
G^{*}
. Its vertices now correspond to cylinders (and thus inherit the auxiliary labeling) and the faces correspond to singularities of
q
.

To count square-tiled surfaces with
N
squares (say) the main challenge is to count the number of integer metrics that can be assigned to the ribbon graph
G
. For this purpose the boundary components of
G
are given an auxiliary labeling, and the goal is to count the number
F_{G}({\bf b})
of integer metrics with boundary lengths
{\bf b}=(b_{1},\ldots,b_{2c})\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{2c}
, where
c
is the number of cylinders. Equivalently, we can count for each dual ribbon graph
G^{*}
the number
F_{G^{*}}({\bf b})
of integer metrics with vertex parameters
{\bf b}
.
[
23
, Section 3.3]
recalls properties of the function
\operatorname{vp}
that assigns the vertex paramters
{\bf b}
to a given tuples of edge lengths
w(e)_{e\in E(G^{*})}
of a (dual) Ribbon graph
G^{*}
.

Lemma 6.3
.

A dual ribbon graph
G^{*}
together with a pairing of vertices, i.e. a fixed-point free involution
W
on
\{1,\ldots,2c\}
determines a cylinder stable graph
\Delta
with
c=E(\Delta)
edges.

The notation for the involution suggests that we are interested in the count of
F_{G^{*}}({\bf b})
for
{\bf b}
lying on a
wall
, defined as

(62)

W\;=\;\{b_{i}-b_{W(i)}=0\,\,\text{for all $i=1,\ldots,2c$}\}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}_{>0}^{2c}\,.

intentionally abusing the letter
W
.

The strategy can now be summarized as follows:

i)

The volume of the stratum
Q(\mu)
is the sum over all graphs
\Delta
– more precisely over all dual ribbon graphs
G^{*}
with vertex pairing
W
that induce
\Delta
– of the large
N
-asymptotics of
F_{G^{*}}({\bf b})
times height and width parameters that give in total given
N
squares (
[
23
, Proposition 4.1]
).

ii)

The analagous statements holds for abelian strata with a single zero, and products of those strata (
[
23
, Section 4.3]
), which appear in the decomposition below. Both i) and ii) still hold for height and width parameters in a coset of finite index (
[
23
, Lemma 7.1]
).

iii)

The sum over all
G^{*}
of
F_{G^{*}}({\bf b})
is a quasi-polynomial, in fact a polynomial on every coset of
(2\mathbb{Z})^{2c}\subset\mathbb{Z}^{2c}
, whose top term agrees with the Kontsevich polynomials
N_{g,2c}^{\mu}({\bf b})/2
(
[
23
, Proposition 3.9]
).

iv)

The Kontsevich polynomials
N_{g,2c}^{\mu}({\bf b})
can be computed as intersection number of Witten-Kontsevich’s combinatorial classes (
[
23
, Section 2 and Appendix B]
and Section
7
).

However, since we need to evaluate the function
F_{G^{*}}({\bf b})
on walls, the agreement with the Kontsevich polynomial in iii) no longer holds. This is the context of
[
23
, Section 5]
).

We thus
fix the cylinder stable graph
\Delta
with a
fixed auxiliary labeling
of the non-leg half-edges
\{1,\ldots,2c\}
with the involution
W
that pairs them. Our goal is an analog of
[
23
, Theorem 5.1]
, which makes explicit the overcounting of the Kontsevich polynomial compared to the metrices of all (dual) Ribbon graphs that give
\Delta
when paired with
W
.
10
10

10

We aim for this overcounting theorem only in the special case of the walls we need. In loc. cit. more general wall structures are allowed, with the aim of e.g. proving (e.g. “string”) equations for Witten-Kontsevich’s combinatorial classes.

As explained in
[
23
, Proof of Theorem 5.1]
the overcounting stems from integral metrics on all dual ribbon graphs
G^{*}
that are compatible with
(\Delta,W)
and where the edge length
w_{e}=0
for a subset of
e\in E(G^{*})
that are called
degenerating edges
.

Recall that the (global) Ribbon graphs
G^{*}
are non-bipartite, since we consider quadratic differentials that are not squares of an abelian differential. Call
e\in E(G^{*})

static
, if a connected component of
G^{*}-e
is bipartite.
The weight
w(e)
of an edge is uniquely determined by the vertex parameters
{\bf b}
if and only if
e
is static, combine
[
23
, Lemma 3.3 and Lemma A.4]
. As a consequence:

Lemma 6.4
.

The overcounting of a degenerate (dual) Ribbon graph contributes to the top order term of
F_{G^{*}}({\bf b})
if and only if all the degenerating edges are static.

The more precise goal is thus to describe all possible configurations of static degenerating edges on dual Ribbon graphs compatible with
(\Delta,W)
and, for given splitting pieces, count the number of ways to reassamble them to a graph
G^{*}
.

Let
G^{*}_{1},\ldots,G_{h}^{*}
be the connected components of
G^{*}
minus the static edges
E_{\operatorname{stat}}(G^{*})
. Then the
static graph
G_{\operatorname{stat}}^{*}
of
G^{*}
, the graph with vertices
G_{i}^{*}
and edges
E_{\operatorname{stat}}(G^{*})
.

Ribbon graphs to sunflower graphs

If all the static edges of
G^{*}
are bridge edges (i.e. they disconnect
G^{*}
), then we may work with the consequences of
[
23
, Lemma 5.4]
as in the case of
|\mu|\geq 3
, where this the only possibility that occurs.

For the convenience of the reader, we explain how associate with
G^{*}
a sunflower graph
\Gamma
, implicitly hidden in the proof of Theorem
6.1
: the unique non-bipartite component
G_{0}^{*}
of
G^{*}
will be the sun of
\Gamma
. Each face of
G_{0}^{*}
without adjacent trees gives a marking (a leg) at the sun. All the other faces
F
of
G_{0}^{*}
give a bottom level (genus zero) vertex of
\Gamma
with a unique marking of order corresponding to the number of edges adjacent to that face. For each component
G_{i}^{*}
that belongs to a subtree growing from a face
F
of
G_{0}^{*}
as add a top level vertex to
\Gamma
of the corresponding genus and adjacent precisely to the bottom level vertex corresponding to
F
. Also in the case
|\mu|=2
, with this assignment the ribbon graphs all whose static edges are bridges gives precisely the contribution of sunflower graphs in Theorem
6.2
.

In the remainder of the section we consider the complementary case and in particular
restrict to the case
n=|\mu|=2
.
The following replaces
[
23
, Lemma 5.4, Lemma 5.5 and 5.6]
.

Proposition 6.5
.

Suppose that
G^{*}
has a static non-bridge edge
e
. Then the static graph
G_{\operatorname{stat}}^{*}
contains a unique (’main’) cycle (consisting of static non-bridge edges).

Each graph
G^{*}_{i}
is bipartite and has exactly one face. In particular
G_{\operatorname{stat}}^{*}
naturally acquires the structure of a dual of a (planar) Ribbon graph
G_{\operatorname{stat}}
.

Moreover, the vertices of each index pair
(i,W(i))
belong to the same component
G^{*}_{j(i)}=G^{*}_{j(W(i))}
of the static graph.

Proof.

If
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
are two static bridge edges, then
G^{*}-e_{1}-e_{2}
is disconnected (if not, any bicoloring of it leads to a contradiction when inserting the edges). The same argument shows that if there are three static bridge edges, then one of the paths joining the edges has to pass through
e_{2}
. Inductively this shows that all static bridge edges lie on a cycle. The remaining (non-bridge) static edges must then grow from this cycles, by the very definition of ’non-bridge’, giving
G_{\operatorname{stat}}^{*}
the structure of one cycle with dangling trees.

To show that each
G_{i}^{*}
has exactly one face we argue inductively as in
[
23
, Lemma 5.5]
with the leaves of the dangling trees. It thus suffices to prove this property in case
G_{\operatorname{stat}}^{*}
, when the argument is similar: Consider a connected component
G_{i}^{*}
. The two faces adjacent to a static edge
e
that enters the same cannot be equal, since otherwise
e
would be non-bridge. Joining the two half-edges that enter
e
we see that
G^{*}
has at least one more face than
G_{i}^{*}
. Since
G^{*}
has just two of them, the claim follows.

For the last claim and the components of the dangling tree the argument is as in
[
23
, Lemma 5.6]
: Consider a
G_{i}^{*}
which is a leaf. Since the adjacent edge is degenerating and since
G_{i}^{*}
is bicolored in black
B
and white
W
vertices, we must have a relation
\sum_{i\in B}b_{i}=\sum_{i\in W}b_{i}
by
[
23
, Lemma 3.3]
. Such a relating can follow from the defining relations (
62
) only if the claimed condition holds for
G_{i}^{*}
. Now induct on the dimension to the leaf.

We thus may assume that
G_{\operatorname{stat}}^{*}
consists of the cycle only. The claim is trivial if this graph has only one edge, so assume it has two, denoted by
e_{1}
and
e_{2}
. Color
G^{*}-e_{1}
so that this edge is adjacent to two black
B
vertices. The set of black and white vertices splits into two subsets for each of the two components of
G^{*}-e_{1}-e_{2}
, call them
B^{\top},B^{\bot},W^{\top},W^{\bot}
respectively, which are the two components
G_{1}^{*}
and
G_{2}^{*}
in this case. Now
[
23
, Lemma 3.4]
implies

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\;w(e_{1})\;=\;\sum_{i\in B^{\top}}b_{i}+\sum_{i\in B^{\top}}b_{i}-\sum_{i\in W^{\top}}b_{i}-\sum_{i\in W^{\top}}b_{i}

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\;\pm w(e_{2})\;=\;\sum_{i\in B^{\top}}b_{i}-\sum_{i\in B^{\top}}b_{i}-\sum_{i\in W^{\top}}b_{i}+\sum_{i\in W^{\top}}b_{i}

since coloring with respect to
e_{2}
flips the color on one of the two components. Adding and subtracting these equations gives equations that can only be deduced from (
62
) if the claimed conditions hold. The general case follows by applying this argument to two adjacent edges of
G_{\operatorname{stat}}^{*}
.
∎

Ribbon graphs to special star-shaped graphs

We associate to
G^{*}
with a static non-bridge the special star-shaped graph
\Gamma
as follows. By definition there is precisely one bottom level vertex with the two markings and for each component
G_{i}^{*}
(say with
e_{i}
edges) of
G_{\operatorname{stat}}^{*}
we add a top level holomorphic vertex without markings of genus
g_{i}=(e_{i}+1)/2
to
\Gamma
(and enhancement
\kappa_{i}=2e_{i}=4g_{i}-2
).

Next we analyze the converse step, namely how many ways there are to glue the given splitting pieces as in Proposition
6.1
to a given ribbon graph.
Suppose we are given

a)

bipartite dual ribbon graphs
G^{*}_{1},\ldots,G_{h}^{*}
, each with one face, corresponding the minimal abelian stratum in genus
g_{i}
, i.e. with
e_{i}:=2g_{i}-1
edges and thus a face adjacent to
\kappa_{i}=2e_{i}
edges.

b)

a vertex labeling and a fixed-point free involution, such that
(i,W(i))
belong to the same component
G_{j}^{*}
of the dual ribbon graph.

We aim for Proposition
6.1
, which is the analog of
[
23
, Proposition 5.7]
. We first compute the volume of the bottom level strata, to make the goal of the computation apparent. For
a\geq 2
set
f_{2}(a,1)=1/(a+2)
and for
n\geq 2
we let

(63)

f_{2}(a,n)\;=\;\frac{a!!}{(a-2(n-2))!!}

This function appears in volume computations in the following form:

Proposition 6.6
.

The intersection-theoretic volume of the quadratic stratum in for the genus zero signature
\widetilde{\mu}=(m_{1},m_{2},-4g_{1},\ldots-4g_{h^{\mathrm{ab}}})
is

(64)

\operatorname{vol}(\widetilde{\mu})\;=\;\sum_{I\subset\{1,\ldots,h^{\mathrm{ab}}\}\atop\text{such that
${c_{1,I}>0}$}}c_{1,I}f_{2}(m_{1},|I|+1)f_{2}(m_{2},|I^{c}|+1)

where
c_{1,I}=m_{1}+2-\sum_{i\in I}4g_{i}
.

Proof.

The formula is the last (very long) displayed formula in the proof of
[
12
, Theorem 1.2]
.
∎

To see which static graphs can appear with the given data, we fix a specific degeneration
{\bf b}^{\prime}(\epsilon)\to{\bf b}
for
\epsilon\in[0,1]
starting at a point in the complement of all hyperplanes in the wall
W
. That is we relabel the vertices such that
W(i)=i+c
, such that the labels
\leq c
correspond to black vertices and the labels
\geq c+1
are white vertices. Moreover we suppose that

(65)

b_{i+c}^{\prime}-b_{i}^{\prime}=2^{i}\epsilon\,,

i.e., the last tuple has the largest deviation from zero. While a priori the count might depend on the chosen order of equations, it turns out that it actually does not.

Proposition 6.7
.

The number
\mathcal{G}_{h}=R(G_{1}^{*},\ldots,G_{h}^{*},{\bf b}^{\prime})
of ribbon graphs
G
whose degenerating edges as
{\bf b}^{\prime}(\epsilon)\to{\bf b}
form a static graph with pieces as in a), b) and which has precisely one cycle is equal to

(66)

\mathcal{G}_{h}\;=\;\operatorname{vol}(\widetilde{\mu})\cdot\prod_{e\in E(\Gamma)}\frac{\kappa_{e}}{2}

with
\operatorname{vol}(\widetilde{\mu})
as in (
64
)

As part of the proof we describe the possibilities to make out of the vertices
G_{i}^{*}
a graph
G_{\operatorname{stat}}^{*}
with
m
edges, hence exactly one loop, and two faces, and such that moreover those faces are adjacent to
m_{1}+2
and
m_{2}+2
many edges respectively. We call this procedure a
joining
, and subsequently count those joinings. Such a joining is called
admissible
if there is a degeneration
{\bf b}^{\prime}(\epsilon)\to{\bf b}
such that the weight
w(e)
of every static edge is positive for
\epsilon>0
.

The ordering of the vertices such that (
65
) holds induces a natural ordering of the
G_{i}^{*}
, namely by the maximum vertex label
s
contained in
G_{i}^{*}
. For convenience we assume in the sequel that this order is increasing, i.e. that the
G_{i}^{*}
are labeled such that

(67)

\max\{s:b_{s}\in G_{i}^{*}\}<\max\{s:b_{s}\in G_{j}^{*}\}\quad\text{if and only if $i<j$.}

We root the subtrees in
G_{\operatorname{stat}}^{*}
at the point where they join the unique cycle and call
descendents
of
G_{i}^{*}
the vertices not on the path to the root.

Lemma 6.8
.

A joining is weakly admissible if and only if the following four conditions hold:

•

Let
G^{*}_{\max}
be the vertex of main cycle of
G_{\operatorname{stat}}^{*}
that contains the largest wall equation inside or whose branch contains this wall equation. Then the two non-bridge degenerating edges adjacent to
G^{*}_{\max}
end at vertices of the same color. Define this color to be black.

•

For all other pieces of the cycle the two adjacent non-bridge degenerating edges end at vertices of different color.

•

If all descendants of
G_{i}^{*}
have labels smaller than
i
, then the path from
G_{i}^{*}
to its root emanates at a black corner of
G_{i}^{*}
.

•

Otherwise the path from
G_{i}^{*}
to its root and the path to the subtree containing the descendent of maximal label are at vertices of different colors of
G_{i}^{*}
.

A joining is admissible if the two faces of the resulting graph
G^{*}
are adjacent to
m_{1}+2
and
m_{2}+2
edges.

Proof.

Consider how the edge lengths are computed from the vertex parameters
[
23
, Lemma 3.3]
, given that these are uniquely determined for static edges by
[
23
, Lemma 3.2]
. The last two statements can be derived exactly as in
[
23
, Lemma 6.2]
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.1
.

Given an admissible joining with the induced coloring for each vertex
G_{i}^{*}
, whose face has
\kappa_{i}=4g_{i}-2
edges, the coloring rules of Lemma
6.1
only allow to rotate all the attached graphs in steps of two, preserving the coloring. This gives
e_{i}=\kappa_{i}/2
choices and we isolate this rotation factor
R=\prod_{i=1}^{h}e_{i}
.

The graph
G_{\operatorname{stat}}^{*}
has two faces corresponding to the zeroes
z_{1},z_{2}
. The vertices
G_{i}^{*}
’s are divided into three disjoint sets: those fully adjacent to the face
z_{1}
that we will index by
I
, those fully adjacent to
z_{2}
that we index by
J
, and those adjacent to both faces forming the disconecting cycle that we will index by
K
. The perimeter of the disconnecting cycle adjacent to the face
z_{1}
has length
c_{1,I}=m_{1}+2-\sum_{i\in I}4g_{i}
, and that adjacent to the face
z_{2}
has length
c_{2,J}=m_{2}+2-\sum_{j\in J}4g_{j}
. Then, for such a
G_{\operatorname{stat}}^{*}
to exist, it must be satisfied that
c_{1,I},\ c_{2,J}>0
and
K\not=\emptyset
. We denote by
\mathcal{N}(I,J,K)
the number of such admisible joinings divided by the rotation factor
R
. Then the total number of admisible joinings is the sum

\mathcal{G}_{h}\;=\;R\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\sqcup J\sqcup K\;=\;\{1,.\ldots,h\}\\
c_{1,I},\ c_{2,J}>0\\
K\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\mathcal{N}(I,J,K)\,.

We proceed to compute the number
\mathcal{N}(I,J,K)
. Assume
G^{*}_{h}=G^{*}_{\max}
. The vertices indexed by
I
form a (possibly disconnected) tree. The number of these trees is given as a factor of equation (38) of
[
23
, Theorem 5.1]
, which we denote by the function
f_{2}(m_{1},|I|+1)
. Analogously, the number of trees formed by the vertices indexed by
J
is given by
f_{2}(m_{2},|J|+1)
. The ways to attach these pair of trees to the main disconnecting cycle depends on the position of
G_{h}^{*}
. If
h\not\in I
, there are
c_{1,I}
ways to attach the tree to main cycle. Analogously, if
h\not\in J
, there are
c_{2,J}
ways to attach the corresponding tree. Otherwise, the ways to attach these trees, and the ways to attach the vertices indexed by
K
in the main cycle, is given by the following two cases:

Case 1: Suppose
h\in I
or
h\in J

The coloring rules give a special role to the vertex to whom the branch containing
G_{h}^{*}
is attached. We call it the
special vertex

G_{t}^{*}
and reserve the symbol
t\in K
for it. Fix an order of the vertices in the cycle. By the first coloring rule on Lemma
6.1
, the number of edges of
G_{t}^{*}
facing the
z_{1}
-face is even, say
2l_{t}
. By the third and fourth coloring rules, the branch containing
G_{h}^{*}
must be attached to one of the
l_{t}
white corners of
G_{t}^{*}
. For the rest of the vertices
G_{i}^{*}
with
i\in K/\{t\}
, the second coloring rule says that the number of edges is odd, say
2l_{i}-1
. Then fix a tuple
\{l_{i}\}_{i\in K}
with
1\leq l_{i}\leq e_{i}
and
\sum_{i\in K}l_{i}=(c_{1,I}-1)/2
. By the coloring rule the number of ways to join a given tree formed out of the vertices of
I
is equal to
l_{i}
, if
i=t
is the special vertex. Then the total number of ways (over all
t\in K
) to join the labeled vertices in
K
up to rotation with a given
I
-vertex tree becomes

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{cycle}\;=

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i\in K}l_{i}\right)\#\left\{\{l_{i}\}_{i\in K}\ |\ 1\leq l_{i}\leq e_{i},\ \text{and}\ \sum_{i\in K}l_{i}=(c_{1,I}-1)/2\right\}

\displaystyle\;=

\displaystyle\frac{c_{1,I}-1}{2}\left[t^{\frac{c_{1,I}-1}{2}}\right]\prod_{i\in K}(t+\ldots+t^{e_{i}})

\displaystyle\;=

\displaystyle\frac{c_{1,I}-1}{2}\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{L\subseteq K}\binom{(c_{1,I}-3-D_{L})/2}{|K|-1}

where
D_{L}=\sum_{i\in L}\kappa_{i}
and where the star restricts the sum to terms where the numerator of the binomial coefficient is not smaller than the denominator. To rewrite the expression using
c_{2,J}
in the interior sum we use the variables
r_{i}=e_{i}-l_{i}+1
so that

\displaystyle\#

\displaystyle\{\{l_{i}\}_{i\in K}\ |\ 1\leq l_{i}\leq e_{i},\ \text{and}\ \sum_{i\in K}l_{i}=(c_{1,I}-1)/2\}

\displaystyle=\;\#\{\{r_{i}\}_{i\in K}\ |\ 1\leq r_{i}\leq e_{i},\ \text{and}\ \sum_{i\in K}r_{i}=(c_{2,J}+1)/2\}

and the expression gets rewritten as

\mathcal{K}_{cycle}\;=\;\frac{c_{1,I}-1}{2}\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{L\subseteq K}(-1)^{|L|}\binom{(c_{2,J}-1-D_{L})/2}{|K|-1}\,,

where the star expresses again the condition
(c_{2,J}-1-D_{L})/2\geq|K|-1
or equivalently that
c_{2,J}-1-D_{L}\geq 2(|K|-1)
. We multiply this count by the
(|K|-1)!
ways to permute the vertices in the cycle, and by the
2^{|K|}
ways of coloring them, as we removed only the factor
R
but according to the coloring rules, vertices in the circle can be joined at vertices black-to-white or vice versa the say clockwise order. We obtain that

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{N}(I,J,K)_{h\in I}}{c_{2,J}f_{2}(m_{1},|I|+1)f_{2}(m_{2},|J|+1)}

\displaystyle=\;2^{|K|}(|K|-1)!\mathcal{K}_{cycle}

\displaystyle=\;(c_{1,I}-1)\sum_{L\subseteq K}(-1)^{|L|}[c_{2,J}-1-D_{L}]_{|K|}\,.

where we use the bracket notation for

(68)

[a]_{r}:=\textbf{1}_{a\geq 2(r-1)}f_{2}(a,r+1).

Symmetrically, if
h\in J
, the count in terms of
c_{2,J}
becomes

\frac{\mathcal{N}(I,J,K)_{h\in J}}{c_{1,I}f_{2}(m_{1},|I|+1)f_{2}(m_{2},|J|+1)}\;=\;(c_{2,J}-1)\sum_{L\subseteq K}(-1)^{|L|}[c_{2,J}-3-D_{L}]_{|K|}\,.

Case 2: Suppose
h\in K

The special vertex in the cycle is now
G_{h}^{*}
, where
2l_{h}
is the number of edges facing the
z_{2}
-face. Let
2l_{i}-1
be the number of edges facing the
z_{2}
-face for every
i\in K/\{h\}
. Then, fixing an order of the vertices of the cycle, the number of attachments is

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{cycle}\;=

\displaystyle\#\{\{l_{i}\}_{i\in K}\ |\ 0\leq l_{h}\leq e_{h},\ 1\leq l_{i}\leq e_{i}\ \text{for}\ i\in K/\{h\},\ \sum_{i\in K}l_{i}=(c_{2,J}-1)/2\}

\displaystyle\;=

\displaystyle\#\bigl\{\{l_{i}\}_{i\in K}\ |\ 1\leq l_{i}\leq e_{i},\ \sum_{i\in K}l_{i}=(c_{2,J}-1)/2\bigr\}

\displaystyle+\#\bigl\{\{l_{i}\}_{i\in K/\{h\}}\ |\ 1\leq l_{i}\leq e_{i},\text{for}\ i\in K/\{h\},\ \sum_{i\in K/\{h\}}l_{i}=(c_{2,J}-1)/2\bigr\}

\displaystyle\;=

\displaystyle\sum_{L\subseteq K}(-1)^{|L|}\binom{(c_{2,J}-3-D_{L})/2}{|K|-1}+\sum_{L\subseteq K/\{h\}}(-1)^{|L|}\binom{(c_{2,J}-3-D_{L})/2}{|K|-2}

\displaystyle\;=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L\subseteq K\\
h\in L\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|L|}\binom{(c_{2,J}-3-D_{L})/2}{|K|-1}+\sum_{L\subseteq K/\{h\}}(-1)^{|L|}\binom{(c_{2,J}-1-D_{L})/2}{|K|-1}

where the last sum is given by the binomial theorem. Multiplying by the
(|K|-1)!
ways to permute the vertices, and the
2^{|K|-1}
ways of coloring the vertices (as the coloring of
G_{h}^{*}
is fixed) the total count of joinings up to rotation is given by

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{N}(I,J,K)_{h\in K}}{c_{1,I}c_{2,J}f_{2}(m_{1},|I|+1)f_{2}(m_{2},|J|+1)}\;=\;2^{|K|-1}(|K|-1)!\mathcal{K}_{cycle}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}L\subseteq K\\
h\in L\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|L|}[c_{1,I}-3-D_{L}]_{|K|}+\sum_{L\subseteq K/\{h\}}(-1)^{|L|}[c_{1,I}-1-D_{L}]_{|K|}.

again with the bracket notation from (
68
).

Conversion of expressions

Using the partition notation our goal is to show that
\mathcal{G}_{h}=\mathcal{F}_{h}
where

\mathcal{F}_{h}\;=\;R\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\sqcup J\sqcup K=\{1,\ldots,h\}\\
c_{1,I},c_{2,J}>0\\
K^{\prime}\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\mathcal{F}(I,J,K)

is defined via

\mathcal{F}(I,J,K)\;=\;c_{1,I}f_{2}(m_{1},|I|+1)c_{2,J}f_{2}(m_{2},|J|+1)\sum_{L\subseteq K}(-1)^{|L|}[c_{2,J}-2-D_{L}]_{|K|}\,.

Note that the components of the expression for
\mathcal{G}_{h}
depend on the location of the maximal element
h
, but the expression for
\mathcal{F}_{h}
don’t.
We write
I^{\prime}\sqcup J^{\prime}\sqcup K^{\prime}
for the partition of
\{1,\ldots,h-1\}
as above after removing the maximal element
h
. The computation above gave us to the following expressions:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(I^{\prime}\cup\{h\},J^{\prime},K^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\;(c_{1,I^{\prime}\cup\{h\}}-1)f_{2}(m_{1},|I^{\prime}|+2)c_{2,J^{\prime}}f_{2}(m_{2},|J^{\prime}|+1)

\displaystyle\cdot\sum_{L\subsetneq K^{\prime}}(-1)^{|L|}[c_{2,J^{\prime}}-1-D_{L}]_{|K^{\prime}|}

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(I^{\prime},J^{\prime}\cup\{h\},K^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\;c_{1,I^{\prime}}f_{2}(m_{1},|I^{\prime}|+1)(c_{2,J^{\prime}\cup\{h\}}-1)f_{2}(m_{2},|J^{\prime}|+2)

\displaystyle\cdot\sum_{L\subsetneq K^{\prime}}(-1)^{|L|}[c_{2,J^{\prime}\cup\{h\}}-3-D_{L}]_{|K^{\prime}|}

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(I^{\prime},J^{\prime},K^{\prime}\cup\{h\})

\displaystyle=\;c_{1,I^{\prime}}f_{2}(m_{1},|I^{\prime}|+1)c_{2,J^{\prime}}f_{2}(m_{2},|J^{\prime}|+1)

\displaystyle\cdot\Bigl(\sum_{L\subseteq K^{\prime}}(-1)^{|L|}[c_{2,J^{\prime}}-1-D_{L}]_{|K^{\prime}|+1}

\displaystyle-\sum_{L\subseteq K^{\prime}}(-1)^{|L|}[c_{2,J^{\prime}}-3-D_{L\cup\{h\}}]_{|K^{\prime}|+1}\Bigr)

All these functions, as well as
\mathcal{F}(I,J,K)
, involve an auxiliary summation over
L\subset K^{\prime}
, and thus so does the error function

\mathcal{E}(I,J,K)\;=\;\mathcal{F}(I,J,K)-\mathcal{N}(I,J,K)\,

where our goal is to show the vanishing of

\frac{\mathcal{F}_{h}-\mathcal{G}_{h}}{R}\;=\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I\sqcup J\sqcup K=\{1,\ldots,h\}\\
c_{1,I},c_{2,J}>0\\
K\not=\emptyset\end{subarray}}\mathcal{E}(I,J,K)\,.

We will use the shorthand notation

\displaystyle A\;=

\displaystyle c_{2,J^{\prime}}-2,\qquad\delta^{+}_{r}(A)\;=\;

\displaystyle[A]_{r}-[A+1]_{r},\qquad\delta^{-}_{r}(A)\;=

\displaystyle[A]_{r}-[A-1]_{r}\,.

For a fixed partition
I^{\prime}\sqcup J^{\prime}\sqcup K^{\prime}=\{1,\ldots,h-1\}
, there are three types of error functions depending on the location of
h
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(I^{\prime}\sqcup\{h\},J^{\prime},K^{\prime})\;=

\displaystyle c_{2,J^{\prime}}\,f_{2}(m_{1},|I^{\prime}|+2)\,f_{2}(m_{2},|J^{\prime}|+1)

\displaystyle\cdot\sum_{L\subseteq K}(-1)^{|L|}\left(c_{1,I^{\prime}\cup\{h\}}\delta^{+}_{|K^{\prime}|}(A-D_{L})+[A-D_{L}+1]_{|K^{\prime}|}\right)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(I^{\prime},J^{\prime}\sqcup\{h\},K^{\prime})\;=

\displaystyle c_{1,I^{\prime}}f_{2}(m_{1},|I^{\prime}|+1)f_{2}(m_{2},|J^{\prime}|+2)

\displaystyle\cdot\sum_{L\subseteq K}(-1)^{|L|}\left(c_{2,J^{\prime}\cup\{h\}}\delta^{-}_{|K^{\prime}|}(A-D_{L}-4g_{h})+[A-D_{L}-4g_{h}-1]_{|K^{\prime}|}\right)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(I^{\prime},J^{\prime},K^{\prime}\sqcup\{h\})\;=

\displaystyle c_{1,I^{\prime}}c_{2,J^{\prime}}f_{2}(m_{1},|I^{\prime}|+1)f_{2}(m_{2},|J^{\prime}|+1)

\displaystyle\cdot\sum_{L\subseteq K}(-1)^{|L|}\left(\delta^{+}_{|K^{\prime}|+1}(A-D_{L})-\delta^{-}_{|K^{\prime}|+1}(A-D_{L}-d_{h})\right)\,.

We denote these three functions as
\mathcal{E}_{*}(I^{\prime},J^{\prime},K^{\prime})
for
*=I,J,K
respectively and denote by
\mathcal{E}_{*}(I^{\prime},J^{\prime},K^{\prime})_{L}
the summand for fixed
L
in each of them. We regroup these sums by defining

L^{\prime}=J^{\prime}\sqcup L,\qquad H=K^{\prime}\setminus L\;=\;(I^{\prime}\sqcup L^{\prime})^{c},

and hence
K^{\prime}=H\sqcup L
. Observe that if we fix
L^{\prime}
, the argument
A-D_{L^{\prime}\setminus J^{\prime}}=c_{2,L^{\prime}}-2+2|L^{\prime}\setminus J^{\prime}|
only depends on the cardinality
|J^{\prime}|=\colon j
. If we let

R_{*}(I^{\prime},L^{\prime},H)=\sum_{J\subseteq L^{\prime}}\mathcal{E}_{*}(I^{\prime},J^{\prime},K^{\prime})_{L^{\prime}}

the sums become

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{I}(I^{\prime},L^{\prime},H)\;=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J^{\prime}\subseteq L^{\prime}\\
K^{\prime}\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|L^{\prime}\setminus J^{\prime}|}\,c_{2,J^{\prime}}\,f_{2}(m_{1},|I^{\prime}|+2)\,f_{2}(m_{2},|J^{\prime}|+1)\cdot

\displaystyle\cdot\Bigl(c_{1,I^{\prime}\cup\{h\}}\,\delta^{+}_{|K^{\prime}|}\big(A-D_{L^{\prime}\setminus J^{\prime}}\big)+\big[A-D_{L^{\prime}\setminus J^{\prime}}+1\big]_{|K^{\prime}|}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{J}(I^{\prime},L^{\prime},H)\;=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J^{\prime}\subseteq L^{\prime}\\
K^{\prime}\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}(-1)^{|L^{\prime}\setminus J^{\prime}|}\,c_{1,I^{\prime}}\,f_{2}(m_{1},|I^{\prime}|+1)\,f_{2}(m_{2},|J^{\prime}|+2)\cdot

\displaystyle\cdot\Bigl(c_{2,J^{\prime}\cup\{h\}}\,\delta^{-}_{|K^{\prime}|}\big(A-D_{L^{\prime}\setminus J^{\prime}}-4g_{h}\big)+\big[A-D_{L^{\prime}\setminus J^{\prime}}-4g_{h}-1\big]_{|K^{\prime}|}\Bigr)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{K}(I^{\prime},L^{\prime},H)\;=

\displaystyle\sum_{J^{\prime}\subseteq L^{\prime}}(-1)^{|L^{\prime}\setminus J^{\prime}|}\,c_{1,I^{\prime}}\,c_{2,J^{\prime}}\,f_{2}(m_{1},|I^{\prime}|+1)\,f_{2}(m_{2},|J^{\prime}|+1)

\displaystyle\cdot\Bigl(\delta^{+}_{|K^{\prime}|+1}(A-D_{L^{\prime}\setminus J^{\prime}})-\delta^{-}_{|K^{\prime}|+1}(A-D_{L^{\prime}\setminus J^{\prime}}-(4g_{h}-2))\Bigr)\,.

Using Lemma
6.1
these functions reduce to

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{I}(I^{\prime},L^{\prime},H)\;=

\displaystyle f_{2}(m_{1},|I^{\prime}|+2)\Bigl(c_{1,I^{\prime}\cup\{h\}}\alpha^{0}_{L^{\prime},H}(0)-(c_{1,I^{\prime}\cup\{h\}}-1)\alpha^{0}_{L^{\prime},H}(1)\Big)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{J}(I^{\prime},L^{\prime},H)\;=

\displaystyle c_{1,I^{\prime}}f_{2}(m_{1},|I^{\prime}|+1)\Bigl(\alpha^{1}_{L^{\prime},H}(2-4g_{h})-\alpha^{1}_{L^{\prime},H}(1-4g_{h})\Bigr)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{K}(I^{\prime},L^{\prime},H)\;=

\displaystyle c_{1,I^{\prime}}f_{2}(m_{1},|I^{\prime}|+1)\Bigl(\alpha^{1}_{L^{\prime},H}(0)-\alpha^{1}_{L^{\prime},H}(1)

\displaystyle-\alpha^{1}_{L^{\prime},H}(2-4g_{h})+\alpha^{1}_{L^{\prime},H}(1-4g_{h})\Bigr)\,.

As the
\alpha
-operators have the special values

\displaystyle\alpha^{0}_{L^{\prime},H}(0)

\displaystyle=\;0,\quad

\displaystyle\alpha^{0}_{L^{\prime},H}(1)

\displaystyle=\;-[c_{2,L^{\prime}}-1]_{|H|}f_{2}(m_{2}-c_{2,L^{\prime}}-1,|L^{\prime}|+1)

\displaystyle\alpha^{1}_{L^{\prime},H}(0)

\displaystyle=\;0,\quad

\displaystyle\alpha^{1}_{L^{\prime},H}(1)

\displaystyle=\;-[c_{2,L^{\prime}}-1]_{|H|+1}f_{2}(m_{2}-c_{2,L^{\prime}}-1,|L^{\prime}|+1)

the sum of the three functions become

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(I^{\prime},L^{\prime},H)

\displaystyle\coloneq\,\mathcal{R}_{I}(I^{\prime},L^{\prime},H)+\mathcal{R}_{J}(I^{\prime},L^{\prime},H)+\mathcal{R}_{K}(I^{\prime},L^{\prime},H)

\displaystyle=\;f_{2}(m_{1},|I^{\prime}|+1)[c_{2,L^{\prime}}-1]_{|H|}f_{2}(m_{2}-c_{2,L^{\prime}}-1,|L^{\prime}|+1)

\displaystyle\cdot[(m_{1}-2|I^{\prime}|+2)(c_{1,I^{\prime}\cup\{h\}}-1)+c_{1,I^{\prime}}(c_{2,L^{\prime}}-2|H|+1)]\,.

The last step is now to prove the vanishing of

\frac{\mathcal{F}_{h}-\mathcal{G}_{h}}{R}\;=\;\sum_{I^{\prime}\sqcup L^{\prime}\sqcup H=\{1,\ldots,h-1\}}\mathcal{R}(I^{\prime},L^{\prime},H)\,.

For this, we fix
L^{\prime}
and let
U=\{1,\ldots,h-1\}\setminus L^{\prime}
. It is sufficient to show that

(69)

\sum_{I^{\prime}\sqcup H=U}\mathcal{R}(I^{\prime},L^{\prime},H)\;=\;0\,.

As the common factor
f_{2}(m_{2}-c_{2,L^{\prime}}-1,|L^{\prime}|+1)
is independent on
I^{\prime}
and
H
, this is equivalent to show the vanishing of the sum

\displaystyle S_{L^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\coloneq\,\sum_{I^{\prime}\sqcup H=U}f_{2}(m_{1},|I^{\prime}|+1)[c_{2,L^{\prime}}-1]_{|H|}

\displaystyle\cdot[(m_{1}-2|I^{\prime}|+2)(c_{1,I^{\prime}\cup\{h\}}-1)+c_{1,I^{\prime}}(c_{2,L^{\prime}}-2|H|+1)]\,.

Here we use the same trick of summing over all
I^{\prime}\subseteq U
by first fixing the cardinality of
|I^{\prime}|=i
. Then the above sum becomes

\displaystyle S_{L^{\prime}}\;=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{|U|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I^{\prime}\subseteq U\\
|I^{\prime}|=i\end{subarray}}f_{2}(m_{1},i+2)[c_{2,L^{\prime}}-1]_{|U|-i}(c_{1,I^{\prime}\cup\{h\}}-1)

\displaystyle+f_{2}(m_{1},i+1)[c_{2,L^{\prime}}-1]_{|U|-i+1}c_{1,I^{\prime}}\,.

By using the identities

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I^{\prime}\subseteq U\\
|I^{\prime}|=i\end{subarray}}c_{1,I^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\;\binom{|U|-1}{i}(m_{1}-2i+2)+\binom{|U|-1}{i-1}(c_{1,U}+2|U|-2i)

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I^{\prime}\subseteq U\\
|I^{\prime}|=i\end{subarray}}(c_{1,I^{\prime}\cup\{h\}}-1)

\displaystyle=\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I^{\prime}\subseteq U\\
|I^{\prime}|=i\end{subarray}}c_{1,I^{\prime}}-(4g_{h}+1)\binom{|U|}{i}

we obtain

\displaystyle S_{L^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\;\sum_{i=0}^{|U|}\,\,f_{2}(m_{1},i+2)[c_{2,L^{\prime}}-1]_{|U|-i}\cdot

\displaystyle\cdot\Bigg(\binom{|U|-1}{i}(m_{1}-2i+2)+\binom{|U|-1}{i-1}(c_{1,U}+2|U|-2i)-(4g_{h}+1)\binom{|U|}{i}\Bigg)

\displaystyle+f_{2}(m_{1},i+1)[c_{2,L^{\prime}}-1]_{|U|-i+1}\,\cdot

\displaystyle\cdot\Bigg(\binom{|U|-1}{i}(m_{1}-2i+2)+\binom{|U|-1}{i-1}(c_{1,U}+2|U|-2i)\Bigg)\,.

We substitude
c_{1,U}\;=\;4g_{h}-c_{2,L^{\prime}}
and
\binom{|U|}{i}\;=\;\binom{|U|-1}{i}+\binom{|U|-1}{i-1}
and
merge the terms with the common binomial coefficient and conclude that
S_{L^{\prime}}=0
.
∎

Lemma 6.9
.

The operator

\displaystyle\alpha_{L^{\prime},H}^{\epsilon}(u)\,:=\,\sum_{J^{\prime}\subseteq L^{\prime}}(-1)^{|L^{\prime}\setminus J^{\prime}|}c_{2,J^{\prime}}f_{2}(m_{2},|J^{\prime}|+1)[A-D_{L^{\prime}\setminus J^{\prime}}+u]_{|K^{\prime}|+\epsilon}\,.

has the simpler expression

\alpha_{L^{\prime},H}^{\epsilon}(u)\;=\;-u\,[c_{2,L^{\prime}}+u-2]_{|H|+\epsilon}\,f_{2}(m_{2}-c_{2,L^{\prime}}-u,|L^{\prime}|+1)\,.

Proof.

By fixing
|J^{\prime}|=j
, this operator becomes

\displaystyle\alpha_{L^{\prime},H}^{\epsilon}(u)\;=\;\sum_{j=0}^{|L^{\prime}|}\alpha_{L^{\prime},H}^{\epsilon}(u;j).

with

\displaystyle\alpha_{L^{\prime},H}^{\epsilon}(u;j)\,\coloneq\,(-1)^{|L^{\prime}|-j}f_{2}(m_{2},j+1)[A-D_{L^{\prime}\setminus J^{\prime}}+u]_{|K^{\prime}|+\epsilon}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J^{\prime}\subseteq L^{\prime}\\
|J^{\prime}|=j\end{subarray}}c_{2,J^{\prime}}\,.

Let
r=|L^{\prime}|-j
. Using the identities

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J^{\prime}\subseteq L^{\prime}\\
|J^{\prime}|=j\end{subarray}}c_{2,J^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\;\binom{|L^{\prime}|-1}{j}(m_{2}+2-2j)+\binom{|L^{\prime}|-1}{j-1}(c_{2,L^{\prime}}+2r)

\displaystyle[A-D_{L^{\prime}\setminus J^{\prime}}+u]_{|K^{\prime}|+\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\;[c_{2,L^{\prime}}+u-2]_{|H|+\epsilon}f_{2}(c_{2,L^{\prime}}+2(r-1)+u,r+2)

and the Vandermonde convolution identity (with
2
-step factorials instead of the usual factorials)

\displaystyle\sum_{r=0}^{|L^{\prime}|-1}

\displaystyle(-1)^{r}\binom{|L^{\prime}|-1}{r}f_{2}(m_{2},|L^{\prime}|+1-r)f_{2}(c_{2,L^{\prime}}+u+2r-2,r+2)

\displaystyle=\;f_{2}(m_{2}-c_{2,L^{\prime}}-u,|L^{\prime}|+1)

the claim follows.
∎

7.
Examples and outlook

In the introduction we presented an example illustrating our main theorem that the DR-completed volume agrees with the ribbon graph completed volume for quadratic differentials, i.e. for
k=2
. In this section we present another example for abelian differentials illustrating Theorem
5.7
that gives the DR-completed volume as a graph sum of top tautological intersection numbers.

Moreover we give an outlook comparing the two possible ways to compute completed volumes by evaluations in the tautological ring.

The stratum
\mu=(4,2,-2)
with
k=1

Since this stratum has three zeros there are no special star graphs and the possible list of sunflower graphs for this stratum is as follows:

These contributions add up to

(70)

\overline{\operatorname{vol}}^{\mathsf{DR}}(\mu)\;=\;1/960

which is confirmed by the
DR_cycle
-method (with
chiodo_coeff=True
) of the sage-package
admcycles
.

Two expresssions in the tautological ring

Our main Theorem
1.1
shows that the completed volume can be computed by two quite different expressions in the tautological ring that we briefly compare.

First, the Ribbon graph completed volume for
k=2
and
\mu
with only odd entries is computed as follows as a sum over all stable graphs
\Delta
as in Section
6.1
with edge factors and vertex factors as follows. Let
{\bf b}=(b_{e})_{e\in E(\Delta)}
be a multi-index and
{\bf d}_{v}
be a multi-index

(71)

\overline{\operatorname{Vol}}(\mu)\;=\;\sum_{\Delta}\mathcal{Z}\Bigl(\prod_{e\in E(\Delta)}b_{e}\cdot\prod_{v\in V(\Delta)}\sum_{|{\bf d}_{v}|=\dim(\mu_{v})}2^{g-\dim(\mu_{v})}\langle\tau_{\bf d}\rangle_{\mu_{v}}\frac{{\bf b}^{{\bf d}_{v}}}{{\bf d}!}\Bigr)

using the linear operator
\mathcal{Z}:{\bf b}^{\bf d}\mapsto\prod d_{i}!\zeta(d_{i}+1)/(\sum(d_{i}+1))!
and where

(72)

\langle\tau_{\bf d}\rangle_{{\bf m}}\;=\;\int_{W_{{\bf m},n}}\prod_{i\geq 0}\psi_{i}^{d_{i}},\qquad{\bf d}=(d_{1},\ldots,d_{n})

is the integral over Witten-Kontsevich’s combinatorial classes
W_{{\bf m},n}\subset{\mathcal{M}}_{g,n}
, whose dimension we denoted by
\dim({\bf m})
, see
[
23
, Section 2.1]
,
[
38
,
31
]
.

Second, the DR-completed volume can be evaluated using the Chiodo-class expression in Theorem
2.4
and (
15
). It is also a sum over stable graphs, but in its present form it is not very efficient to evaluate. One reason for this is the dependence on the auxiliary weighting parameter
r
. We conjecture that the Chiodo-coefficient needed here, of top codimension is actually a monomial in
r
, which already gives significant computational simplification. Moreover, the DR-completed volume is defined for any
k
and any
\mu
and exhibits interesting piecewise polynomial behaviour that further improves evaluation. We plan to address this in a sequel to the paper and hope that at least one of the two expressions is suitable to approach the large genus limit conjecture
[
1
]
.
